Spellgarden will release a Switch version of cozy thrift shop management game Thrifty Business, the developer announced.

Thrifty Business first launched for PC via Steam on May 18, followed by iOS and Android on November 10. Here is an overview of the game, via its Steam page:AboutThere’s a brand new thrift store in town, and guess what? You’re in charge!In Thrifty Business, you’ll set up and manage your very own 90s-inspired vintage thrift shop. Here is an overview of the game, via its Steam page:

There’s a brand new thrift store in town, and guess what? You’re in charge!

In Thrifty Business, you’ll set up and manage your very own 90s-inspired vintage thrift shop. Search through boxes filled with pre-loved gems and use them to create gorgeous displays. Unlock furniture, decorations, wallpaper & floors to create a beautiful community space that brings everybody together!

Adjust your layout and organize your shelves and racks to the finest detail. Group your wares by color, theme, purpose, or whatever you like. The more organized your store is, the more community points you’ll get to unlock new goodies!

Customize the furniture, wallpaper, flooring, and decor to make your store as welcoming and cozy as possible. As business grows, you might even need to expand into the adjacent rooms!

This is so much more than just a store. Listen to customer’s stories and try to fulfill their requests. You can also unlock and host hobbyist events, turning your business into a treasured third space for locals!

Arc’teryx Rethinks Retail With a Climbing-Focused LA Store

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

For most retailers, a climbing wall would be an unusual addition to the sales floor. For Arc’teryx, it became the starting point for an entirely different kind of store. The Canadian outdoor brand recently opened its first climbing-focused retail concept on Abbot Kinney Boulevard in Venice, California, rethinking the traditional shopping experience around climbing culture, creativity, and community.

Rather than simply dedicating more floor space to climbing equipment, Arc’teryx designed the location as a place where people could gather, exchange knowledge, and spend time together.

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

“We started this new concept with a desire to reimagine traditional retail as a community-driven experience hub,” explains Whitney Kirk, Director, Future Concepts at Arc’teryx. “Inspired by the climbing ethos of ‘sharing beta’—exchanging knowledge, insights and experiences—we set out to create a space that brings people together, builds meaningful connections and deepens their relationship to the brand.”

That philosophy informed everything from the layout and materials to the furniture and programming.

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

For Senior Retail Designer Kirsty Marsland, the existing building provided an opportunity to establish a distinct architectural identity without relying on overt references to climbing. Original timber beams and columns remain exposed throughout the interior.

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

Those elements are complemented by a deliberately restrained selection of materials. Galvanized steel draws inspiration from the texture of granite rock faces, while Douglas fir plywood brings warmth to the interior. Red upholstery and furniture provide occasional bursts of color, drawing from Arc’teryx’s signature Dynasty red.

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

“The materials aren’t intended to mimic the crag directly,” Marsland explains. “Instead, the crag is the conceptual starting point for the design—a place to gather that feels informal, joyful, and dynamic. It’s this spirit that shapes the space, rather than any literal reference to the crag itself.”

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

The climbing wall itself occupies a prominent position near the front of the store, establishing an immediate connection between the activity inside and the street beyond. Developed in collaboration with Grasshopper, the wall was integrated into the surrounding millwork rather than treated as a separate installation.

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

Local climbing gym Long Beach Rising helped develop the routes, arranging the holds with an attention to composition that gives the wall a sculptural quality. Some of the holds are even made from clay.

That emphasis on local collaboration extends to the Beta Lounge, where furniture by Los Angeles design studio Waka Waka offers a place to sit, read, and connect.

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

“We were looking for light, movable furniture that we could rearrange easily when using the climb wall,” Kirk explains. After meeting Waka Waka founder Shin Okuda and his wife and creative collaborator, Kristen Dickson-Okuda, Kirk says the team knew they’d found the right fit. “They were so open and really understood what we were trying to do with the space,” she adds.

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

That adaptability extends to the architecture itself. Tables on the retail floor can be repositioned for workshops, gatherings, and other programming, while an outdoor patio provides additional room for events and screenings.

An existing wall was sandblasted to expose the brick beneath and reveal its natural texture. “It brings a softness and raw tactility to the space,” Kirk says. “It offers a visual pause, provides a canvas for our Arc’ Presents films, and lets the building’s history become a part of the story.”

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

Arc’ Presents is a rotating series of climbing films and related content. Elsewhere, displays combine athlete photography with artifacts, books, and personal narratives that explore the preparation and experiences behind significant climbs. Climbing guidebooks and copies of Alpinist and Summit Journal in the Beta Lounge give visitors another reason to linger.

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

While the Abbot Kinney location is Arc’teryx’s fourth store in Los Angeles, it represents a departure from the brand’s established retail format. Beyond its specialized assortment, the differences extend to the materials, fixtures, circulation, and approach to storytelling.

The concept also suggests a broader shift in how physical retail spaces can operate. Rather than treating community programming as an occasional addition to shopping, Arc’teryx has made it a defining part of the architecture. The climbing wall, movable furniture, and gathering spaces support activities that don’t necessarily involve making a purchase.

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

For Kirk, that adaptability is essential to the store’s long-term success. “Our approach was to create a flexible, multi-purpose environment that could evolve with the needs of the local community.”

Whether gathering for a film screening, exchanging climbing advice, or spending an afternoon reading in the lounge, visitors have plenty of reasons to stop by beyond shopping. For Arc’teryx, the culture of climbing has become the foundation for a different kind of retail experience.

Photography by Julian Berman; courtesy of Arc’teryx

ApeChain migrates to Chainlink CCIP for APE and USDC transfers – Details

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

ApeChain is replacing LayerZero with Chainlink CCIP. This move comes as the layer 3 blockchain aims to strengthen its infrastructure and improve connectivity across the Yuga Labs ecosystem.

The migration makes CCIP the official cross-chain standard for ApeChain, Bored Ape Yacht Club applications, and APE.

Additionally, it will be migrating the ChainLink Cross-Chain Token (CCT) that is used by the CCT implementation of APE and USD Coin [USDC], among others. This is to enable programmable transfers from these tokens without having to use LayerZero’s former OFT.

Therefore, this may provide easier access to the decentralized application and community space across the Ethereum [ETH], Arbitrum, Base, and Polygon chains. As well, Chainlink has described CCIP as a single secure cross-chain standard.

The expanded reach of Chainlink CCIP provides a larger scale significance for the ApeChain integration. Chainlink CCIP processes over $25 billion in cumulative transfer volume.

It also currently supports 60 to 77 different networks and has sent over 1.63 million messages. Previously, it was integrated with BitGo’s WBTC, Wyoming’s FRNT, and other assets from Kelp and Mantle.

These previous integrations have moved over $15 billion worth of token value via CCIP in 2026. In addition to providing additional support to this expanding network of users, Yuga Labs will also bring a large, consumer-focused, and NFT-centered ecosystem into play.

Ultimately, Rising transfers, repeat activity, deeper liquidity, and competitive fees will show whether adoption delivers lasting growth.

While CCIP is continuing to expand its reach, it needs to continue translating this reach into sustainable revenue. More importantly, CCIP recently underwent its first upgrade (CCIP 2.0) since September 2026.

Additionally, integrations with Aave [AAVE], Lombard, and Venus demonstrate CCIP’s continued involvement in the development of DeFi. While CCIP continues to add to its involvement in DeFi infrastructure, commercial returns for the project remain limited.

This gap represents the difficulty in transitioning from simply being involved in infrastructure adoption into generating consistent economic returns.

ApeChain has adopted Chainlink CCIP as its cross-chain standard for APE and other ecosystem assets.

Chainlink’s CCIP adoption is growing, but low fee revenue remains a challenge.

Muriuki Lazaro is a on-chain data analyst with a B.Sc. in Data Science. Muriuki specializes in dissecting complex on-chain data into clear and accurate insights for readers in the crypto ecosystem, with a particular focus on Bitcoin.

Why a Mathematician Is Chasing Eclipses With Algorithms and Cameras

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The November issue of IEEE Spectrum is here!

With custom-built gear, his team seeks to solve a long-standing solar mystery

Advanced image-processing software developed by Miloslav Druckmüller combines scores of individual images, captured during a total solar eclipse and painstakingly calibrated. The techniques reveal fine details in the sun’s corona, which is white in this image. The tiny pink shapes seen along portions of the edge of the blacked-out solar disk are prominences, which are extensions of relatively cool plasma that can jut out hundreds of thousands of kilometers into space.

The sun hangs low over the plains of Castile and León, in the north-central region of Spain. But from a slope of the mountain Pico Trigaza, it appears that a few puffy white clouds have started to block the sun. That’s vexing.

A group of researchers, some wearing cardboard eclipse glasses, is clustered around sets of portable telescopes and cameras connected to laptops. All this gear is scattered on tabletops perched on the mountainside. On the computer screens, the sun’s disk looks like a deep orange crescent that is slowly waning. That’s because the moon began to crawl across it about half an hour ago. In 25 minutes, it will obscure the sun completely, producing an eerie and wondrous phenomenon—a total solar eclipse.

In the late-summer, late-afternoon heat, the researchers watch anxiously as those fluffy clouds linger around the sun. From an abandoned hut on the slope a few meters above, where the researchers have set up camp, one of the grad students emerges and nods toward the cloud formation. “Do you think it will dissolve?” he blurts out.

“It will.” Miloslav Druckmüller glances calmly at his group of doctoral candidates, now making final checks of the equipment in preparation for totality. Here in Spain, on this 12th day of August, Druckmüller is getting ready for his 19th solar eclipse. He knows from long experience that weather can turn quickly when the moon’s shadow suddenly pushes the temperature down. Plus, there’s no sense in riling up the grad students any more than they already are.

Miloslav Druckmüller led the design and construction of a unique optical instrument that incorporates eight astrocameras. Each is equipped with a spectral filter for sensing and recording specific emission spectra from the solar corona. Each of those spectra, in turn, is associated with a particular ionic species of iron atoms. Because the temperature needed to ionize iron varies depending on the ionic species, analyzing the locations and relative abundance of these ionic species offers valuable insights into the distribution of heat in the corona.Václav Široký

During totality, the moon blocks the overwhelming glare of the solar disk, revealing delicate structures in the sun’s outer atmosphere, the corona. Using custom-built telescope systems and image-processing techniques that Druckmüller has spent decades developing, the researchers aim to gain insights into the processes in this enigmatic region that is otherwise hard to study. In addition to imaging the visible coronal light, the team will try to track the dispersal of superhot ions of iron into interplanetary space, which could give rare insights into the pattern of heat emanating from the corona.

And they’ll have 107 seconds to do all that.

Druckmüller is a professor of mathematics and image processing at the Brno University of Technology in the Czech Republic and a leading figure in the Solar Wind Sherpas, an international team that studies the sun’s corona during total eclipses. In the solar eclipse world, he is nothing short of a legend, albeit a reluctant one.

An image from Miloslav Druckmüller’s first solar-eclipse expedition, to Hungary, vividly shows Baily’s Beads. These elongated white lights along a piece of the left edge of the blacked-out solar disk are caused by beams of sunlight shining through gaps in the irregular lunar surface during the eclipse. The pink shapes are solar prominences, which are jets of plasma extending out into space and held in place by the sun’s intense magnetic field.

He photographed his first solar eclipse in 1999, in what he thought would be a one-off lark. During that eclipse, the moon’s deep shadow missed Druckmüller’s native Czechia by just a few tens of kilometers, so he chased it to Hungary with a 30-year-old Soviet MTO 1,000-millimeter lens. All he remembers from that day is the rush of adrenaline he felt while trying to find the sun in the sky with the lens’s minuscule field of view and keep it in focus for the two minutes of the eclipse.

He insists that he has never enjoyed any of the eclipses he has photographed. “In fact, I have never properly seen any of those eclipses. You are just watching the screens, making sure that it’s all doing what it’s supposed to do. There are so many things that can go wrong and you can’t redo it.”

A composite sequence of photographs shows the 12 August 2026 solar eclipse as seen from a slope of the mountain Pico Trigaza in Spain.

Despite Druckmüller’s outward tranquility on this Spanish hillside, the stakes are high. Just 45 minutes ago, his group heard from their colleagues in Iceland, where viewing conditions had promised to be better than in Spain. But a vast low-pressure system had clouded the entire sky, leaving all three Iceland teams without a view.

On the Pico Trigaza mountain in Spain, two postgraduate researchers used a laser to get a rough, initial alignment for the tracking mount of an optical instrument. The alignment was necessary to precisely compensate for the Earth’s motion.

The corona has been a mystery for centuries. Though dimmer—by a factor of a million—than the sun’s visible surface, the corona is nevertheless extremely hot: about a million degrees Celsius compared to 5,500 degrees on the surface. Because the sun’s nuclear fusion engine churns at the star’s heart, it might seem that temperatures should fall farther from the core.

To observe the faint corona in visible light, researchers must block the sun’s ferocious glare. Specialized telescopes called coronagraphs do this with an opaque disk called an occulter. But because light bends around the occulter’s edges in an effect called diffraction, it must block an area much larger than that of the solar disk to prevent light from spilling over and ruining the images. But doing so means obstructing the inner part of the corona, which harbors the most important processes that underpin the puzzling phenomena of the sun’s upper atmosphere.

That’s why total eclipses matter so much to solar physicists. By sheer chance, the moon is exactly the right size and at just the right distance to completely cover the sun during a total solar eclipse. Because the moon is so far away from Earth, the view is not marred by diffraction, and the corona reveals itself nearly all the way down to the sun’s surface.

As astrocameras recorded spectral data about the sun’s corona, a laptop computer [on table, at left] displayed the phase of the eclipse.

Space probes can image the solar surface in other wavelengths, but those views have limitations, too. Spacecraft such as NASA’s Solar Dynamics Observatory do see the innermost parts of the corona in extreme ultraviolet light, explains Shadia Habbal, a solar physicist at the University of Hawaii and leader of the Solar Wind Sherpas project. “But it cuts off very quickly, so you don’t see its extension into space as well as we can see it in the visible emission lines” during an eclipse. “That’s the biggest advantage,” she says, of solar eclipse photography.

The European Proba-3 mission, launched in 2024, simulates total solar eclipses by flying two spacecraft in a formation 150 meters apart with one acting as an occulter and the other as the telescope. The instruments aboard that satellite, however, have limitations compared to the equipment used by the Solar Wind Sherpas team.

When Druckmüller developed the film from his 1999 eclipse expedition, the photographs didn’t do justice to the event’s eerie beauty. He spent countless evenings applying his expertise in numerical image processing in an attempt to re-create in his photographs the sights he witnessed in that Hungarian corn field.

It took two years, but eventually he succeeded. Where before there were dull views of the blackened lunar disk surrounded by the blunt wisps of the corona, Druckmüller revealed the subtlest details of the magnetic field lines stretching from the sun far into space. As his photographs circulated among solar physicists, invitations to eclipse expeditions began to arrive.

Solar researchers Miloslav Druckmüller and Shadia Habbal have been collaborating for two decades and 15 eclipses.

Habbal learned about the images from a Ph.D. student who had seen them at a conference presentation that didn’t credit Druckmüller. When Habbal’s team later published the images in a paper, they misattributed them to another researcher. Druckmüller found out through an astrophysicist friend. “I wrote her a rather disappointed email and she responded within 10 minutes,” Druckmüller recalls. “She really didn’t have a clue.”

The error was corrected, and within weeks, Habbal and Druckmüller began a collaboration that grew into a friendship. “Milos’s processing brings out details that nobody has seen before, and they tell you a lot about very tiny features in the corona that you don’t see otherwise,” says Habbal.

Capturing the corona’s delicate structures means finding a way to show its brightest and faintest features in the same image. “During the solar eclipse, the contrast between the darkest and brightest parts of the corona is enormous, possibly one to a million,” Druckmüller explains. “But a computer monitor only allows you to display a contrast ratio of one to about 800.” (Some modern monitors, particularly OLED ones, can deliver higher contrast ratios.)

The human eye can adjust to this enormous range of brightness, allowing an observer to see both the bright corona and the faint stars and planets that briefly emerge in the darkened sky. To bring those details together in a photograph, Druckmüller uses custom image-processing algorithms to combine exposures that capture different parts of that range.

Researchers Martin Šebek and Peter Urgoš make fine adjustments to the focus and alignment of the astrocameras on a key optical instrument before the 12 August solar eclipse.

To create a single image, Druckmüller’s teams need to take 200 individual frames during the eclipse at exposure times ranging from 0.001 second to two seconds. The data must then be calibrated against a vast number of reference images to filter out noise and imperfections. Flat fields, which depict a uniform light background, help correct for defects caused by telescope optics, dust spots, and variations in pixel sensitivity. Dark frames, taken with light blocked from the sensor, help correct for artifacts created by the camera’s image sensor itself. “It takes the computer more than two days to process one image,” Druckmüller says. Including the calibration data, a single finished image requires about 3,000 input frames.

Since 2008, teams directed by Habbal and Druckmüller have traveled to every total solar eclipse, spreading out across multiple locations to hedge against bad weather. About half of the teams return with usable data. For the others, it’s just a short and stressful voyage. For the 12 August eclipse, three groups deployed to Iceland and two to Spain.

The teams seek sites close to the center of the band of totality, where the moon blocks the sun for longest. In Spain, the 12 August band of totality cut across the north of the country; the center line of the shadow passed within 11 kilometers of Pico Trigaza, promising Druckmüller and his colleagues a precious 107 seconds of totality.

But duration is only part of the calculation. Totality on Pico Trigaza will commence 51 minutes before sunset, with the sun just 8 degrees above the horizon. That low, the rays must pass through a much thicker layer of the atmosphere than when the sun is higher in the sky, increasing the light distortion that complicates photography. At the researchers’ camp on Pico Trigaza, 1,600 meters above sea level, the thinner air should help a bit.

An abandoned hut on the mountain Pico Trigaza in Spain was spruced up so that it could serve as a home base for the research team recording the 12 August solar eclipse.

“Iceland would have been a much better place for us than Spain because the sun was higher up, some 25 degrees above the horizon,” Druckmüller says wistfully. But Iceland’s notoriously unpredictable weather made Spain an essential backup.

If you pack a van with thousands of dollars’ worth of cameras and optical gear and then set out on a 2,351-kilometer journey, you shouldn’t be surprised if you finish the trip with a few striking photographs [top]. The sleeping arrangements for the grad students at the Pico Trigaza hut [bottom] were spartan, but the views were first-rate.Top: Martin Šebek; Bottom: Václav Široký

On the mountain, locals have spruced up a rustic, long-unused hut for the researchers, running power cables from a nearby telecommunication outpost and bringing in a water tank. Five Ph.D. students drove more than 2,000 kilometers from Brno and have spent nearly a week preparing the equipment. Druckmüller and his wife joined them two days before the eclipse. They cook on a small propane grill and sleep in sleeping bags on yoga mats. “We have plenty of space here to set up our telescopes, beautiful views, and a roof over our heads,” Druckmüller says with a grin. “It’s perfect.”

But even the best equipment and a carefully chosen site count for nothing if the clouds roll in. “So much work goes into the preparations and then, if you end up with nothing, you just feel terrible sadness,” says Jana Hoderová, an assistant professor of mathematics at Brno University of Technology and one of Druckmüller’s key collaborators.

As the moon continues to slide in front of the solar disk, the pesky cumulus cloud that kept everybody on edge begins to dissolve. It’s 10 minutes to totality. The relief is palpable.

The countdown on the computers that control the cameras has begun. The screens are now showing only a tiny orange sliver, and the evening is becoming perceptibly less bright.

Twenty astrophotography cameras, most of them drawing power from a car battery that was also buffering the cameras from any grid instabilities, are pointed at the sun. Alongside equipment capturing the corona’s visible light, the team has built an assembly of eight cameras to detect light emitted by various iron ions that arise in the corona.

Among the optical gear assembled at Pico Trigaza to capture the 12 August solar eclipse was a unique instrument designed to record the distributions of iron ions in the corona [at left]. Václav Široký

Those instruments looking for iron ions have been put together with resourcefulness and thrift. A colleague of Druckmüller’s laser-cut the aluminum plates holding the carefully aligned lenses in exchange “for two bottles of whisky.” The brackets and attachments were 3D-printed at Brno University of Technology. The lenses, produced for the defunct camera maker Contax, were bought at steep discounts on eBay. By far the most expensive elements are interference filters that isolate the wavelengths emitted by the iron ions.

Around the blackened solar disk rises the magnificent golden halo of the corona. In the hush on the mountainside, it seems that even the birds have gone still.

“That’s something nobody else in the world can do,” Druckmüller says with visible satisfaction. The unique technology, codeveloped with Habbal’s Hawaii team, has already enabled researchers to map the distribution of temperatures across the entire corona and to discover previously unseen structures, dubbed “smoke rings,” in the coronal plasma. The photographs also revealed the boundary region where the solar plasma becomes so thin that its ions stop colliding.

The optical assembly designed to record the distributions of iron ions in the corona is outfitted with astrocameras, each of which captures spectra from a different ionic species. To do that, each astrocamera is equipped with a spectral filter whose bandwidth is so narrow—one nanometer—that the filter appears as a mirror.Martin Fornůsek

“Iron is the most abundant of the heavy elements that we see in the solar corona,” says Habbal. “And because the corona is so hot, the iron atoms lose electrons and get ionized. The larger the loss, the higher the temperature.”

For example, a temperature of around 1.6 million °C is necessary to produce an iron ion that has lost 12 electrons. But only 1.4 million °C is needed to strip an iron atom of 11 electrons. Once it’s in a particular electrical state, the ion retains its specific ionization, even as it travels outward with the solar wind. Each of those ions, whether +11 or +12, emits a different wavelength of light, allowing the researchers to map not just temperatures within the corona, but also how those iron ions spread into interplanetary space and distribute heat. Habbal and her colleagues had long been trying to image those iron ions during solar eclipses. But it wasn’t until Druckmüller entered the picture that they were able to develop the gear and the mathematical techniques to do it.

With just a minute to go to totality, the researchers begin removing protective filters from the lenses. It’s 50 minutes to sunset, and the waning sun has just begun to light up the clouds above the horizon with the first tinge of orange. Then, suddenly, the evening begins a rapid and premature descent into darkness. Along the edge of the fantastically thin solar crescent, the brilliant orange points known as Baily’s Beads flash and disappear—the last fragments of photospheric light visible through deep lunar valleys. As totality begins, observers remove their glasses. The hot air cools. The graduate students remain quietly and intently focused on their gear.

Druckmüller looks up from his camera toward the darkened plains. Around the blackened solar disk rises the magnificent golden halo of the corona. In the hush on the mountainside, it seems that even the birds have gone still. The sky is ablaze with fiery colors all along the horizon where the deep lunar shadow drops off. Venus burns brilliantly in the southwest, a sharp white pinpoint in the darkened sky. The sound of camera shutters suddenly fills the air. Then, as quickly as it fell, the darkness begins to recede, bid farewell with another handful of golden Baily’s Beads.

The crowd erupts in cheers. The Ph.D. students throw themselves at each other and hug like they mean it.

“The students did most of the work this time, so I actually allowed myself to really look at it,” Druckmüller says. “It was beautiful. Pico Trigaza may not have been the best for science photography, but from the aesthetic point of view, it was extraordinary.”

The 2026 solar-eclipse team at Pico Trigaza in Spain included [from left] Magdalena Šrotířová, Peter Urgoš, Martin Fornůsek, Miloslav Druckmüller, Václav Široký, and Martin Šebek.Václav Široký

But for Druckmüller, this is just the beginning. While other eclipse chasers will race to release their images, he will take his time. He expects to publish the first visible-light images of the corona in about two weeks. Processing the iron-ion images, and uncovering whatever insights they may hold, will take months—maybe even a whole year.

“Probably until the next eclipse in 2027” in North Africa, Druckmüller says.

Who knows? Maybe he’ll even enjoy that one, too.

Tereza Pultarova is a London-based journalist specializing in aerospace and defense technologies. She is a native of the Czech Republic.

Those are some pretty impressive photos! It also seems like the iron ion filtering is pretty unique. I love seeing cases where people build something they want to see in the world with minimal outside support. Seems like it would be a solid return on more public money to seek out and support scrappy projects like this one.

The midterms and SCOTUS

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

In the aftermath of Election Day, Republicans and Democrats alike are grappling with what the blue wave means for the Supreme Court. Keep reading to learn how the Senate shake-up, in particular, could affect when a justice next leaves the court and how the confirmation process might unfold.

Plus, one year ago today, the justices heard argument in Learning Resources, Inc. v. Trump, a challenge to President Donald Trump’s tariff regime. Just over three months later, the Supreme Court struck down the tariffs, holding that the International Emergency Economic Powers Act does not give the president the authority to impose tariffs.

Tomorrow, the justices will meet in a private conference to discuss cases and vote on petitions for review. Orders from Friday’s conference are expected on Monday at 9:30 a.m. EST.

On Oct. 21, the Trump administration asked the justices to allow it to resume construction of its border wall in the Big Bend region of southwestern Texas. The request is now fully briefed, and the court’s decision could come at any time.

Record-low temperature created on China’s space station

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

A Bose-Einstein condensate reached about 10 trillionths of a kelvin

An experiment on China's Tiangong space station (rendering shown) reached a record-low temperature in a state of matter called a Bose-Einstein condensate.

Shujianyang/Wikimedia Commons (CC BY-SA 4.0)

This is a human-written story voiced by AI. Got feedback? Take our survey . (See our AI policy here .)

Science has reached a new low. Temperature, that is.

Scientists cooled a collection of atoms to about 10 picokelvins, or 10 trillionths of a kelvin. That’s barely a smidge above absolute zero (−273.15° Celsius). The atoms formed a Bose-Einstein condensate, a quantum state of matter, colder than any before, researchers report November 5 in Science.

To achieve that record-low chill, scientists took their work to space. The atoms orbited Earth aboard China’s Tiangong space station. There, researchers used laser-based traps to contain 20,000 rubidium atoms and reduce their motion, thereby cooling them. At an atomic level, temperature is tied to motion: Atoms and molecules in the air around us move more slowly when it’s colder. Similarly, in systems of cold atoms, scientists can define an effective temperature based on how slowly the atoms spread apart when they are allowed to expand.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

On Earth, gravitational forces cause clouds of atoms to sag, reducing the effectiveness of cooling. But on the space station, the atoms experienced microgravity, the apparent weightlessness of an object in free fall.

To make a Bose-Einstein condensate, a disorderly gaggle of atoms is cooled until the atoms all take on the same quantum state, acting as one entity. By experimenting with ultracold atoms, researchers can study exotic states of matter and other mysterious physics. And they can test fundamental concepts, such as the equivalence principle — the idea that explains why objects of different masses fall at the same rate in a vacuum.

Spacefaring Bose-Einstein condensates were previously made on the International Space Station, at effective temperatures as low as 52 picokelvins. And an experiment on Earth, in which atoms were dropped down a tower to induce weightlessness, reached 38 picokelvins. But drop tower experiments are limited by the time their free fall lasts. That’s not an issue for experiments in Earth’s orbit. So cold atoms in space are really taking off.

Nature Communications. 2022. doi: 10.1038/s41467-022-35274-6C. Deppner et al. Collective-mode enhanced matter-wave optics. Physical Review Letters. 2021. doi: 10.1103/PhysRevLett.127.100401

Senior physics writer Emily Conover has a Ph.D. in physics from the University of Chicago. She is a two-time winner of the D.C. Science Writers’ Association Newsbrief award and a winner of the Acoustical Society of America’s Science Communication Award.

Greece Approves 10% Crypto Capital Gains Tax as First Framework Lands Below Draft Rate

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Greece's parliament approved a 10% capital gains tax on cryptocurrencies, the country's first dedicated crypto tax framework, with annual gains up to €500 exempt.

Greece’s parliament approved on Thursday a bill that imposes a 10% capital gains tax on cryptocurrencies, the first comprehensive legal framework for taxing crypto assets in the country.

Cryptocurrency capital gains of up to €500 ($563) a year will be exempt from the new tax, the bill says. For bitcoin holders in Greece, that threshold is a small buffer, not a carve-out for larger positions.

“Further implementation directives will be published in the coming weeks, which will also define transparency and audit rules,” a senior finance ministry official told Reuters.

An earlier draft circulated in October had floated a 15% rate. The version lawmakers passed lands five points lower, while keeping the €500 annual exemption that was already in that proposal.

The tax is set to apply from Jan. 1, 2027, Greek coverage of the finance ministry bill says. It is levied on the net gain — the difference between the acquisition price and the transfer price — not on the full sale proceeds. Trading fees and other costs tied to the deal can factor into that calculation.

A taxable transfer is not limited to selling for euros. Using cryptocurrency to buy goods or services, or contributing it to cover or increase company capital, can also crystallize a gain under the new rules. That matters for anyone in Greece who pays a merchant in bitcoin rather than converting first.

Simply holding bitcoin while its euro value rises does not, by itself, create a tax bill. Crypto-to-crypto swaps, such as bitcoin for another token, are treated as non-taxable events in the explanatory material that accompanied the bill, matching the approach France has used to avoid taxing every hop between coins.

Net annual gains at or below €500 are exempt. Greek analyses of the text say that once net gains clear that ceiling, the 10% rate applies to the full net gain for the year, not only the slice above €500. Losses above €500 in a tax year may be carried forward for up to five years and offset only against future crypto capital gains.

Purchases of crypto assets are also being pulled into Greece’s existing “source of funds” checks from 2027, so buyers may need to show that the euros used to acquire bitcoin or other tokens came from declared income or other lawful capital. The rule does not add a separate purchase tax; it widens the audit trail around how coins were funded.

European Union countries do not share a unified tax system for the sector. Rates on cryptocurrency gains typically run from about 8% to 30% across member states and are usually levied on capital gains, the finance ministry official said. Greece’s 10% sits toward the softer end of that band, below France’s often-cited 30% treatment and above Cyprus’s 8%.

The same patchwork is shifting elsewhere in the bloc. The Netherlands has moved toward a realized capital gains tax from 2028, while Germany and Portugal still exempt crypto held longer than a year. Since January, the EU’s DAC8 reporting rules have required crypto platforms to send user and transaction data to national tax authorities — a tool Greece already wrote into domestic law.

State revenue from the new levy could not be projected, the official said, because the vast majority of investors use platforms abroad, leaving the size of Greece’s cryptocurrency market hard to measure.

For bitcoin holders who trade on foreign exchanges, that gap is the practical test. The rate is now set in law; whether Athens can match 10% on paper with enforceable cost-basis data from offshore platforms is the question the coming directives will have to answer.

Oldest evidence of cannabis use discovered in 2,700-year-old royal tomb

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 5, 2026 2:00 PM EST

The use of cannabis is documented across cultures stretching back thousands of years, but it frequently relates to harvesting the plant for hemp and other non-psychoactive products. That said, one Bronze Age society of horseriders living in present-day Russia called the Scythians may offer the oldest example of intentionally ingesting cannabis for its intoxicating properties. In the Eurasian steppes of Siberia, archaeologists have discovered a princely Scythian tomb complex featuring an estimated 1,000 cannabis seeds intentionally placed among the dead.

“The seeds show that cannabis played an important role in the rituals surrounding the burial,” Gino Caspari, an archaeologist at the University of Bern in Switzerland, explained in a statement.

These weren’t simply symbolic objects, either. As Caspari and his colleagues detail in a study published today in the journal Science, human hair samples from the central burial room indicate Scythian royals also partook in cannabis use.

The Scythians were a group of ancient eastern Iranian nomadic equestrians renowned for their horseback skills. The community migrated from Central Asia into present-day Ukraine and southern Russia beginning in the eight and ninth centuries BCE, and stayed there for roughly the next 500 years. Their regional influence eventually waned before the Scythians ultimately assimilating into neighboring populations after being displaced by the Goths during the third century CE.

The burial complex Caspari’s team examined is located in Tuva, a south Siberian republic of Russia located along Mongolia’s northern border. Although ancient plant material like leaves, flowers, and resin generally decompose long before archaeologists can ever locate them, seeds sometimes endure when shielded from the elements. In this case, the royal tomb ensured near optimal conditions to protect the cannabis remnants. Laboratory radiocarbon dating of six recovered seeds indicate Bronze Age attendants likely placed them inside the burial chamber during the early eighth century BCE.

The exact details of Scythian cannabis cultivation and use remains unclear. Researchers theorize the plants were wild variants that naturally grew in the region. At the same time, hair analysis through methods like high-resolution mass spectrometry revealed the clear presence of cannabinol (CBN), which is produced by the oxidation of cannabis’ primary intoxicant, tetrahydrocannabinol (THC). The team also noted weak signs consistent with THC itself.

The detection of these molecular signals doesn’t reveal how much cannabis the Scythian royals ingested, or the methods they used. At the same time, other Central Asian archaeological sites contained cannabinoid chemical remnants on heated stones and inside vessels, which strongly suggest inhalation.

“The molecular traces in the hair and the cannabis seeds come from the same grave, and suggest that cannabis was used for its intoxicating properties,” concluded Caspari.

Archaeologists are now sequencing the DNA from some of the tomb’s seed samples, which will help them trace the plant’s spread and later human domestication. Cannabis wasn’t the only addition to the royal Scythian tomb, however. Prior work at the site has yielded the skeletal remnants of 18 horses arranged on the burial mound, along with an assortment of human bones.

“In this princely tomb, the burials were accompanied by extensive horse sacrifices and, most likely, human sacrifices as well,” added Caspari.

Nov. 5, 2006: Mars Global Surveyor goes quiet

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Designed to study Mars in a comprehensive way, examining its atmosphere, its interior, and its surface, the Mars Global Surveyor (MGS) launched Nov. 7, 1996. By Sept. 12, 1997, it would reach Mars and slip into orbit around the Red Planet. Over the next nine years, it revealed an evolving world of ongoing geological dynamism, analyzed the history of water on Mars, tracked the movements of ice at its poles, and analyzed its topography and weather. With nearly 200,000 photos captured, MGS supplied more images of Mars than all the missions before it – combined. It also helped NASA plan for future rover missions to the Red Planet.

MGS substantially exceeded its two-year mission, but on Nov. 2, 2006, the orbiter signaled to NASA that it was experiencing difficulties with its solar panels. Two days without communication followed. On Nov. 5, more signals were sent, and then MGS went silent. The mission officially ended on Nov. 14, with NASA concluding a battery failure had caused a computer error that closed out MGS’s tour.

Elisa Neckar is senior production editor of Astronomy magazine, and the editor of the Today in the History of Astronomy feature on Astronomy.com.

Solar carport project at California medical plaza solves tricky energy billing

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Watthub Renewables and Sunrock Distributed Generation completed a 322-kW solar photovoltaic system at Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza, the three-story Class A medical office building in Irvine, California. Solar modules were installed to carports covering approximately 108 parking spots, turning bare asphalt into shaded, covered parking and into the building’s primary source of electricity.

Watthub Renewables, Sunrock Distributed Generation and SunRenu Solar completed a 322-kW carport solar project at Irvine Heritage Medical plaza in Irvine, California. Credit: Watthub Renewables

The system is expected to generate approximately 515,690 kWh in its first year, covering the majority of the plaza’s annual electricity consumption. It was delivered under a 25-year power purchase agreement requiring no upfront capital from Irvine Heritage’s ownership. Watthub and Sunrock DG financed, engineered and contracted by SunRenu Solar. Sunrock DG now owns and maintains the array, while the property buys the power it produces at a rate below Southern California Edison’s.

Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza was built in 2008 on just over two acres, with approximately 40,000 sq ft of medical suite.

“Medical office is one of the few property types where the parking lot is part of the patient experience,” said Aaron Moston, director at Watthub Renewables. “Somebody’s coming out of a medical or dental procedure at two in the afternoon in August. Whether their car is 140 degrees or 95 degrees is not a small thing. Carports were the right structure here before we ever ran the production model — the fact that they also happen to be the best south-facing real estate on the property is what made the project work.”

Irvine Heritage Medical Plaza is master metered, meaning the entire building draws electricity through a single Southern California Edison (SCE) account on the GS-3-TOU commercial rate, and that cost flows through to tenants inside their charges rather than through individual utility bills. For years, that meant every practice in the building carried a share of an electric bill it could not see or control in a utiliyt territory with rate increases and, during the summer, on-peak energy.

Since solar generation is netted at the master meter, the savings are not captured by one tenant — they reduce the building’s total electricity cost.

“A master meter is usually described as a disadvantage — tenants have no visibility into their usage and the owner absorbs every swing in the energy bill,” said Wilson Chang, CEO at Sunrock DG. “For a solar project it’s the opposite. One meter, one interconnection, one bill to offset and every suite in the building benefits automatically. It’s the simplest way to bring solar to a multi-tenant property.”

Earlier this year, developed another 300-kW solar carport project for the Foothill Medical Association in Foothill, California. The system was also commissioned with a PPA.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

Jack Grealish is EXILED by Everton: Ex-England star is still being left out, confirms boss David Moyes, after boozy nights at pubs and karaoke

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

By HARRY BAMFORTH, LIVE SPORTS COVERAGE REPORTER

Published: 08:52 EDT, 5 November 2026 | Updated: 14:12 EDT, 5 November 2026

David Moyes has confirmed that Jack Grealish will be absent for Everton's clash with Coventry City on Friday following disciplinary action last week - and said he may not even return after the international break.

The 31-year-old was dropped for back-to-back matches against Newcastle following a 'breach of club discipline' and will not be part of the Toffees' matchday squad for the third game on the spin tomorrow night.

'No, Jack is not available,' Moyes said on Thursday. 'I wouldn't give you the plan, but we will be working with Jack again soon.'

Moyes was then questioned by reporters whether the club and Grealish will mend their relationship over the upcoming international break, which is set to span two weeks.

'We'll see,' Moyes replied. 'We need to wait and see how things go.'

Grealish, who has made seven appearances since rejoining Everton for a second season-long loan spell in the summer, has been exiled since failing to show up for a training session on Monday last week. It is understood staff struggled to make contact with the winger.

The Manchester City loanee has since returned to training but is yet to feature for the club since the punishment, having missed both the Carabao Cup 3-1 win over Newcastle and the Premier League victory over the Magpies on Monday.

Jack Grealish remains exiled by Everton - with the winger not available for their game on Friday

Grealish has missed Everton's passed two games after being disciplined for missing training

Grealish had made four successive starts before he missed Everton's 2-0 defeat by Arsenal on October 24 due to illness.

It is thought Moyes had reservations about re-signing Grealish this summer after similar issues last season and last week the manager made it clear that he backed the 'club decision' to discipline the once England international.

Moyes said: 'You probably know I'm not going to give any details, it is a club decision and I support that decision. It is something I don't want to speak too much about.

'The one thing about Everton is that we have got really good values behind it. All the players stand up to the values and accept the values, on the pitch and off the pitch. From that point of view, I think the players did a brilliant job.'

Grealish is reported to have had back-to-back nights out prior to the Toffees' defeat in north London.

The three-time Premier League winner attended the Pride of Britain awards at the Grosvenor House hotel in London on October 19 where he presented the Child of Courage award to 12-year-old Zac Howells.

He is then reported to have finished the night of celebration off at Bam Karaoke Box, a karaoke hotspot in Victoria, central London. According to the Sun, he stayed up until the early hours of the morning singing with his friends and enjoying the drinks on offer.

Grealish is reported to have gone on consecutive nights out after attending the Pride of Britain Awards on October 19

The next day, Grealish is believed to have returned to Cheshire, where he and many of his footballer friends live, to meet team-mates Jordan Pickford and Tyler Dibling at The Mauldeth pub in Wilmslow.

The trio, joined by Grealish's former City team-mate and captain Kyle Walker, where they watched Paris Saint-Germain's thrilling Champions League victory over Barcelona. A witness claimed to the Sun that they enjoyed a few drinks.

Without Grealish once again, Everton are seeking to secure a third win in a row when they come up against former Toffees boss Frank Lampard and his Coventry team.

Their only injury concern ahead of the fixture is Jarrad Branthwaite, who Moyes admitted they have been nursing since he hobbled off late on at St. James' Park with cramp.

The Scot was speaking ahead of his 592nd game in charge of Everton, which will see him draw level with Harry Catterick’s record of the most games managed for the club.

‘I got told about it a few weeks ago,’ said Moyes, who has overseen more than 1,200 games during his coaching career. ‘I didn’t realise I’d got that close.

‘Harry Catterick’s name has always been mentioned alongside Howard [Kendall’s] name. I’m very proud to be up there, to be spoken of in terms of games. I wish I had the trophies to go with what they had.’

Victory over Coventry could see Everton move into the top-four places ahead of the November international break.

‘I think there’s lots to be positive about because of how they [the players] have been doing generally,’ said Moyes. ‘I feel we could have been doing better had we not lost some players last summer. I think it would have kept us right in and amongst it.

‘I think all you can try to do is get as many wins as you can and perform as well as we have. I think the players, in the main, have had good moments in games.’

Key to Everton’s form has been the goals of Thierno Barry. The 24-year-old has netted eight this term, already as many as he managed in his first season at the club.

Asked about the striker’s improvement, Moyes said: ‘I think the first thing is, he is settling down. We took a bit of an early gamble on buying Thierno and maybe knew he wouldn’t be right, right away. That’s been the case.

‘[Erling] Haaland should be worried that he’s just behind him now,’ he joked. ‘He has to be careful, Thierno is on his back.

‘Let’s hope he can keep scoring for us. He keeps trying to improve and I hope he can do that.’

I had an equal partnership – and I still became the default parent

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Home / Relationships / Marriage & Partnerships

Here's how I reclaimed my time without compromising care for my little ones.

By Lais de Oliveira Updated Nov. 05, 2026

Every now and then, my husband picks up his phone and car keys, and announces he’s going out for something spontaneous. Lunch with a friend. A bike ride. He’s gone in about ninety seconds if I don’t stop him at the door.

And I generally don’t, because I want my husband to have time for himself.

Standing behind the closed door, I ask: When was the last time I left this house by myself, without planning it, negotiating it, or trading for it? Not once since my first child was born… three and a half years ago. Then it hits me: I am the proverbial default parent.

In my life, I have moved countries on 48 hours’ notice. I have started businesses and communities on four continents, landed in cities where I knew no one, and figured it out from the ground. Yet here I am, unable to walk out my own front door without a negotiation.

Now I am the kind of person who books a haircut six weeks out.

My husband carries himself with the lightness of someone who isn’t carrying the majority of the parenting load.

The mental one: how many diapers, ounces of milk, and clothing changes need to be packed for a single outing. The emotional one: forecasting ways to address my little one’s growing feelings or managing the conditions to prevent a meltdown. The logistical one: managing energy levels and knowing a missed nap isn’t worth the spontaneity of welcoming last-minute visitors (the hosting of which, by the way, would fall on me).

He doesn’t carry any of that because I do. And I know I’m not alone in this. (There are even several Bluey episodes about it).

But I thought my husband and I would be different. And to his credit, he does a lot for the family, and has always been willing to work towards a situation where I felt supported. Still, for over three years, our day-to-day behaviors set our sails in a different direction; the mere idea that we were working toward a mostly equitable deal blinded me to the fact that, too stereotypically, I was the one carrying the invisible load of parenting.

For a while, I stewed silently, filing away every every “just tell me what to do” and every unplanned walk out the door.

But my resentment wasn’t getting me closer to the future I actually wanted, which is to raise my kids alongside a partner I trust. Anger is honest information, but a terrible operating system. It costs you the very thing you’re angry about losing: your own energy.

So I stopped waiting for space to appear and started claiming it. Here are the steps I took:

The first step was recognizing there was a real problem. Only then could I have a productive, fact-based conversation with my husband.

What helped: talking it out, sharing the situation with friends and watching them nod before I finished the sentence. Those conversations helped clear the fog and made it clear: I had become the default parent without ever applying for the role, and I needed it recognized as a real job, with real requirements, not something invisible and easily taken for granted.

Clarity is a prerequisite for being heard, and it is what moved the conversations with my husband past defensiveness. Until then, the idea that we had a “mostly equitable deal” was blurring his perspective too.

We didn’t get it right at once. Several conversations left one of us hurt, misunderstood, or underappreciated. But we persisted in having the difficult ones. Those conversations eventually gave us enough clarity to lay out, with clinical precision, the ways our partnership had been uneven. We could dissect a messy situation and turn resentment into discernment, naming the areas where I was lacking support and the areas where both of us were lacking appreciation.

Clarity let us stand across from each other, our vision for an equitable partnership leading the way instead of resentment for how it had been.

And we learned something more important: an equitable relationship isn’t found. It’s built. That shared understanding made the next steps possible.

Once we both understood what my resentment had been trying to say, in more positive, proactive terms, my needs became clearer and easier to ask for. And my husband finally had something concrete to act on.

We put our partnership to work: I defined clear steps to reclaim my own freedom, and he redefined what it means to be a supportive partner.

From there, we made a real plan: a family calendar that protects my time the same way it protects his and provides a predictable rhythm. The gym, daily. Tango on Monday nights. A weekday or two blocked out because I’m in the city for meetings. An evening for dinner with a friend.

And, every so often, a half-day on the weekend for my own creative retreat, which is where I am as I type this: in a literal room of my own, my office and studio, with a door that closes.

I still won’t leave the house on a whim. But neither will he. We plan ahead, tell the other person, and arrange childcare if we both want some time out.

Relationships tarnish under unmanaged resentment. Bringing back the shine takes work.

From my end, I had to listen more, or proactively ask for his perspective on an issue I’d raised, and work to transmute resentment into something more productive: naming my unmet need. I had to really care about his perspective, and trust that he had been going for “supportive partner” all along, even if our early attempts had been clumsy and left scars.

From his end, he needed to become more observant and make efforts to show up more often in ways that were meaningful to me: offering to hire help for things previously expected to come from me, checking in before making plans on his own, and looping me in before inviting friends and family.

We also started scheduling more romance, whether a babysitter-powered date or quality time at home. But I’d say this came as a consequence of everything above: there was no mood for a date while our relationship was permeated by resentment. The difficult conversations had to come first.

The work most mothers do is largely invisible. But invisible isn’t the same as involuntary.

I can’t undo how the default got set, or how my own behavior kept reinforcing it. What I can do is refuse to let the default compromise my own health, my relationship, and the example I’m setting for my children. I want them to have the courage to create a life they’re proud to call theirs.

Some of that courage looks like walking out your own front door on a Saturday, keys in hand, for no reason at all: knowing the little ones are safe, and well cared for, because you made it so.

Many parents fear that AI is taking over their teens’ ability to think for themselves. But with some open communication, you can raise kids that are aware of AI’s risks and rewards.

I thought I was giving my kids everything. Instead, I was missing the moments I worked so hard to create.

American Airlines Dropped Trump’s Name From West Palm Beach Gate Screens Ahead Of Midterm Elections

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

American Airlines quietly removed the Trump reference from its gate monitors for flights to West Palm Beach. The timing will invite speculation, but the simpler destination label is an improvement regardless of what prompted the change, which does not appear politically-motivated to me.

JonNYC reports that American has changed its destination displays from “Pres DJT, W Palm Beach” back to “West Palm Beach, FL.”

That reverses a change introduced after Palm Beach International Airport was renamed President Donald J. Trump International Airport earlier this year.

so… has anyone noticed that AA has changed the gate monitors from “Pres DJT, W Palm Beach” back to “West Palm Beach, FL” ?

The change is likely not connected to this week’s election and occurred in August as part of a planned transition. The IATA code for the airport is still DJT after the July 9, 2026 change from PBI.

The original change was peculiar because American generally identifies destinations by city, adding airport names when useful to distinguish between airports serving the same destination.

American does not ordinarily add the presidential names associated with airports serving places like Little Rock (Bill Clinton) or Grand Rapids (Gerald Ford). The West Palm Beach label stood out. Then again, as I indicated above, other airports did not change their IATA code.

There is a very practical reason to distinguish New York JFK from New York LaGuardia or Washington Reagan (National) from Washington Dulles. Leading with presidential initials on a flight to West Palm Beach offers less obvious help to passengers.

There was no possibility of similar confusion when flying into West Palm Beach.

Of course people will notice that this reversal surfaced around the midterm elections, but as I mentioned this goes back to August. Even so, it is an irresistible invitation to speculate about corporate America adjusting to a changed political environment, which was becoming clear even over the summer.

But I think it’s safe to say that American’s leadership did not order a “political” retreat, no matter what some MAGA world may be suggesting this morning. This was more likely all part of the transition plan.

I would be equally cautious about declaring that the original wording was personally ordered by executives seeking favor with the administration, though that could well be the choice as well. It was an unusual choice. The reasoning behind it remains a separate question.

Still, American created an unnecessary distraction by departing from a straightforward city label in the first place. Once a company makes a conspicuous change involving a political figure, undoing it will inevitably attract attention too…especially now.

American Airlines has restored the West Palm Beach destination label on its gate monitors, removing the presidential reference it added earlier this year.

The timing will generate political interpretations. Without more information, I would leave those as speculation and it appears wholly unrelated. The practical result is easier to assess: passengers once again see the name of the place they are flying to. That works for me. Now it’s time to move DJT back to PBI…there was certainly no need for that change either, though that ship (plane?) has sailed.

HR thinks you can learn AI skills – so why is your company hiring elsewhere?

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Over 90% of HR professionals say they believe employees can learn AI roles - but half of companies still hire outside for those positions, according to a new report.

HR leaders are confident that current employees can be trained for jobs created by artificial intelligence. Organizations are hiring externally instead.

That’s the finding in new research from Careerminds, a global provider of workforce solutions. The study, published in Careerminds’ white paper, The AI Redeployment Gap: What Companies Lose by Hiring over Reskilling, reveals that 92% of HR professionals and 88% of people managers believe employees within their organizations could be reskilled for AI-related roles.

Also: Nearly 70% of workers use AI regularly now – but many get no time to upskill

Yet 51% of HR professionals and 47% of people managers report that their organizations are hiring externally for new AI-related positions, compared with just 42% in each group who report redeploying existing employees.

The research was conducted in partnership with Censuswide and surveyed 595 US HR professionals and 547 US people managers, ages 25–65, working at organizations with 500-10,000 or more employees. The research was conducted May 15–26, 2026.

Also: Businesses finally seeing AI ROI, but 62% can’t handle the storage demands

“Companies are investing in AI while potentially overlooking one of their most valuable resources: the people they already employ,” said Mark Saddic, senior partner at Careerminds, in a press release.

“Our research shows that organizations largely believe their employees can adapt to AI-driven change, but that confidence isn’t consistently translating into opportunities. Before looking outside the organization, leaders need to understand the skills they already have, identify where employees can grow, and establish a clear process for making those transitions possible.”

Reskilling, especially for AI, isn’t easy to track. Limited visibility into individual skill sets and which employees should be reskilled was cited as a potential barrier by 43% of HR professionals and 34% of managers.

Also: Pearson’s Workera deal targets a big workplace problem: No time to AI upskill

It’s not the first time that the management of skills development has come up regarding AI. In September, educational giant Pearson agreed to acquire Workera, the AI-native skills platform used by organizations such as ServiceNow, Accenture, and the US Space Force.

Careermind’s data revealed that 87% of HR pros and 84% of managers believe their organizations are adequately considering reskilling employees for future roles. However, the findings suggest that these organizations may believe they are prioritizing internal talent development, even though their hiring practices reveal otherwise.

Response to AI-related workforce needs isn’t equal across the board. The findings reveal significant differences in how industries are responding to these needs.

Also: Your AI-polished resumes are recruiters’ new top stressor

For example, in the technology and telecom industries, external hiring outpaces redeployment by 15%. At the same time, media and entertainment is the only sector surveyed in which both groups report redeployment at least as frequently as external hiring. Among HR professionals in that industry, 56% report redeploying employees, compared with just 25% who hire externally.

Separate research from The Josh Bersin Company, cited in the Careerminds white paper, reveals that external hiring can cost three to five times more than filling comparable roles internally — and on average takes up to 20 days longer.

Time to take Portugal seriously again? Stats since 2022 show why Cristiano Ronaldo retirement could be a blessing

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Cristiano Ronaldo has announced his retirement from international football after 23 years with Portugal. But is the national side actually stronger without him? We’ve crunched the numbers.

Ronaldo bowed out as his country’s record goalscorer, having scored 146 goals in 234 appearances. In his retirement announcement, he spoke of “handing back the number seven shirt” after more than two decades representing his country. Portugal fans can bet on soccer at Bovada.

In 234 international appearances, he scored 146 goals and provided 45 assists, averaging a goal contribution every 97.4 minutes.

With those sorts of numbers, it begs the question of why people would think that Ronaldo is now a hindrance to his country, rather than one of their biggest assets.

This narrative started to gain prominence around the 2022 World Cup, when CR7 found himself in a rut for both club and country.

He only scored one goal during that tournament and his understudy, Goncalo Ramos, was looking sharper at the time.

This led to Ronaldo being benched for Portugal’s quarter-final clash against Morocco, which they ultimately lost 1-0.

Many predicted that was the end for Ronaldo on the international stage, but that couldn’t have been further from the truth.

Roberto Martinez put Ronaldo front and centre, leading to two underwhelming tournament performances and early exits.

Ronaldo has now finally called time on his international career, with his announcement following a turbulent period involving new manager Jorge Jesus.

With a lot of discourse currently around Ronaldo and Portugal, we’ve compared the national team results with and without him since the 2022 World Cup.

Since then, Portugal have played a total of 49 matches. Of those, CR7 has featured in 37 and has missed 12.

In the games Ronaldo has played, Portugal have boasted a win percentage of 70.2% and have scored an average of 2.05 goals per game.

Without Ronaldo, Portugal’s win percentage is higher at 75% and their goal-per-game ratio jumps to 3.41.

Of course, the sample size of games without Ronaldo is small, meaning that the odd freak result will have a larger impact on the numbers.

For example, Ronaldo didn’t feature in Portugal’s 9-0 hammering of Luxembourg back in 2023, which boosts the numbers quite a bit in this regard.

But the direction of travel in late 2026 is clear. Here’s the full breakdown of what the stats say:

Win percentage: 70.2% Loss percentage: 13.5%

Goals per game: 2.05 (78) Goals against per game: 0.63 (24) ⭐

Win percentage: 75%⭐ Loss percentage: 8.3%⭐

Goals per game: 3.41 (41)⭐ Goals against per game: 1.08 (13)

Cristiano Ronaldo merely the latest example of a great player who couldn’t let go | Jonathan Wilson

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

There is no doubt his international career was an incredible one, but it would have been greater still if he had not lingered on the stage so long

Nothing, perhaps, is quite so difficult for an elite sportsperson as timing their exit. It’s not easy for any person in any job to weigh the hope of having more to give with the desire not to linger beyond their usefulness, but it’s perhaps particularly hard for top athletes because unrealistic self-confidence is so essential to their success. It takes an almost unhealthy level of self-belief for anybody to make the sacrifices to get to the top, to dedicate themselves to such a precarious vocation, to overcome all the setbacks and injuries and failures. How can someone – whose whole life has been spent assuring themselves there are no odds they cannot overcome – accept that is it, that the time has come to say goodbye?

The news that Cristiano Ronaldo has retired from international football brings with it, inevitably, a sadness. Sadness we will no longer see him skipping past a defender, or burying a neck-thrusting header, and sadness in that the end of the career of any great is a reminder of mortality; all things in time must pass. But there is also a sadness he timed his departure so badly. For at least three tournaments, there has been a sense of Ronaldo holding Portugal back.

At the 2022 World Cup, Fernando Santos belatedly took the decision to leave Ronaldo out for the last-16 tie against Switzerland. Portugal suddenly had a fluency and fluidity they had lacked for years and the player who had replaced Ronaldo, Gonçalo Ramos, scored a hat-trick. That seemed incontrovertible evidence the end was upon Ronaldo, or at least his role might become that of an impact substitute.

But even in the final minutes of that game, half the crowd were chanting for Ronaldo. Some, perhaps, were Portuguese fans but many were tourists. They didn’t care much about the result or who went through, they just wanted to see the famous man do the famous thing. Near the end, he scored a goal that was obviously offside. He knew it was offside, but he also knew what his public wanted. So he did the “Siuuuu …” celebration and was rewarded with the biggest cheer of the night. This was like seeing late-period Elvis in Las Vegas: it didn’t matter he was no longer the singer he had been, he was still Elvis. It’s just that Elvis wasn’t also carrying Portugal’s World Cup hopes on his rhinestoned shoulders.

When a player has such celebrity status, when he is still somehow the show no matter how sluggish his movements, making that decision on when to quit must become even harder. And Ronaldo, with his status as Portugal’s greatest soft-power export, also became a political issue. A cabinet minister publicly said he couldn’t be dropped because of how much revenue he generated and what he meant for Portugal’s brand. Portugal is not a country replete with global superstars.

Portuguese Football Federation "Every drop of sweat, every decisive goal, and every captain’s arm-band he wore with honour on the field reflected his unconditional love ⁠for our jersey. His dedication ⁠and his legacy ​transcend the four lines of the field. Ronaldo has raised Portugal’s profile and inspired generations of young people who dream of following in his footsteps; for ⁠many of us, he is the greatest player of all time! …"

Al-Nassr (Ronaldo's current club) "It’s not just a chapter – it’s history you’ve shaped. You’re an icon to the entire world, not ⁠just Portugal. Portugal is in your heart, and you are in the hearts of the world. A page ​has turned, but there is still history left ‌for us to write together at ‌your home, Al-Nassr."

Manchester United (one of Ronaldo's former clubs) "Congratulations on an incredible international career."

Bruno Fernandes (Portugal and Manchester United) "Thank ‌you for the inspiration. It was a privilege to be able to share great moments with our national team alongside my childhood idol."

Diogo Dalot (Portugal and Manchester United) "They don’t know that dreams are a constant in life … Portugal’s history tells us that we are a people who have never accepted limits, unafraid of uncharted seas or weathering storms. We were and are shaped by achievements, sacrifices, tears, and sweat … ‌By dreams that seem impossible – until someone has the courage to make them a reality. You did it. You made us believe, dream, and achieve! A legacy that will be remembered ​not only for the trophies or the goals, but also for the path you paved for all those who came after you. Your passion and determination to represent our country is something I’ll never forget and that will stay with me forever. I’m immensely proud of everything you’ve done for our national team! Thank you, idol."

Pepe (Former Portugal and Real Madrid defender) "It was more than just soccer. Thanks for everything, Cris."

Gianni Infantino, Fifa president "Few players have ever ​touched the heart of football fans ​around the world quite like Cristiano. A career defined by extraordinary talent, relentless determination and ​an unwavering desire to ‌push the boundaries ​of what is possible … Cristiano has ​achieved milestones that may never be matched but his greatest legacy goes far beyond records, trophies and goals … Thank you, Cristiano, for the passion, the unforgettable moments and the immense contribution you have made to our beautiful game proudly wearing the 'Selecao' shirt. Your international career will forever be part of football’s history." Reuters

Ronaldo hung around at Euro 2024, weeping after missing a penalty against Slovenia, the magnitude of his fame and ego warping perception so everything became about him, no matter how many other hugely gifted players were in that squad. And implausibly he was still doing it at the World Cup this summer: gumming the mechanisms, constantly demanding the ball, occasionally drifting out to the left to get in Rafael Leão’s way. At one point against Spain in what turns out to have been his final World Cup game, Ronaldo was released on the break but, lacking pace, ended up having to turn back and play the ball to his full-back, the opportunity gone. There was something almost painful about seeing a player once so explosive reduced to this.

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

There needed to be some sort of intervention. This generation of Portuguese players, who really should be challenging for major honours and not just the Nations League, could not continue to struggle on with such an albatross around their necks. It came from Jorge Jesus, the admirably forthright 72-year-old national coach, who was appointed after the World Cup. He didn’t bring Ronaldo off the bench in the second half of a tight game Portugal ended up winning against Norway. Ronaldo insisted he had been promised minutes – but even if there had been an agreement, it would be absurd to refuse a coach the capacity to adapt to circumstance – and stormed out of the camp, an indication perhaps of how his self-regard had come to distort his judgment.

And, assuming this retirement isn’t some sort of power play to force Jesus out, that is where it ended, with a €255 (£212) fine and 30-day ban over a period in which Portugal had no games. Ronaldo will go back to the Saudi Pro League to continue his pursuit of a 1,000th goal. He has been an extraordinary player. He will be remembered as an all-time great, vying with Eusébio as the greatest Portugal player of all time, but his legacy is undoubtedly tainted by this protracted farewell, by the sense that a player who at his best could win games single-handed, came by the end to be a burden to his national team. His has been a great career, but it would have been greater had it been a little shorter.
