Umberto Eco’s Antilibrary: Why Unread Books Are More Valuable to Our Lives than Read Ones

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

“It is our knowledge — the things we are sure of — that makes the world go wrong and keeps us from seeing and learning,” Lincoln Steffens wrote in his beautiful 1925 essay. Piercingly true as this may be, we’ve known at least since Plato’s famous Allegory of the Cave that “most people are not just comfortable in their ignorance, but hostile to anyone who points it out.”. Although science is driven by “thoroughly conscious ignorance” and the spiritual path paved with admonitions against the illusion of thorough understanding, we cling to our knowledge — our incomplete, imperfect, infinitesimal-in-absolute-terms knowledge — like we cling to life itself.

And yet the contour of what we know is a mere silhouette cast by the infinite light of the unknown against the screen of the knowable. The great E.F. Schumacher captured this strange dynamic in the concept of adaequatio — the notion that “the understanding of the knower must be adequate to the thing to be known.” But how do we face our inadequacy with grace and negotiate wisely this eternal tension between the known, the unknown, the knowable, and the unknowable?

That’s what Lebanese-American scholar, statistician, and essayist Nassim Nicholas Taleb explores in a section of his modern classic The Black Swan: The Impact of the Highly Improbable (public library) — an illuminating inquiry into the unknowable and unpredictable outlier-events that precipitate profound change, and our tendency to manufacture facile post-factum explanations for them based on our limited knowledge.

Taleb uses legendary Italian writer Umberto Eco’s uncommon relationship with books and reading as a parable of the most fruitful relationship with knowledge:

The writer Umberto Eco belongs to that small class of scholars who are encyclopedic, insightful, and nondull. He is the owner of a large personal library (containing thirty thousand books), and separates visitors into two categories: those who react with “Wow! Signore professore dottore Eco, what a library you have! How many of these books have you read?” and the others — a very small minority — who get the point that a private library is not an ego-boosting appendage but a research tool. Read books are far less valuable than unread ones. The library should contain as much of what you do not know as your financial means, mortgage rates, and the currently tight real-estate market allows you to put there. You will accumulate more knowledge and more books as you grow older, and the growing number of unread books on the shelves will look at you menacingly. Indeed, the more you know, the larger the rows of unread books. Let us call this collection of unread books an antilibrary.

Eco himself has since touched on humanity’s curious relationship with the known and the unknown in his encyclopedia of imaginary lands, the very existence of which is another symptom of our compulsive tendency to fill in the gaps of our understanding with concrete objects of “knowledge,” even if we have to invent them by the force of our imagination. Taleb adds:

We tend to treat our knowledge as personal property to be protected and defended. It is an ornament that allows us to rise in the pecking order. So this tendency to offend Eco’s library sensibility by focusing on the known is a human bias that extends to our mental operations. People don’t walk around with anti-résumés telling you what they have not studied or experienced (it’s the job of their competitors to do that), but it would be nice if they did. Just as we need to stand library logic on its head, we will work on standing knowledge itself on its head.

Noting that his Black Swan theory centers on “our misunderstanding of the likelihood of surprises” because we underestimate the value of what we don’t know and take what we do know “a little too seriously,” Taleb envisions the perfect dancer in the tango with knowledge:

Let us call this an antischolar — someone who focuses on the unread books, and makes an attempt not to treat his knowledge as a treasure, or even a possession, or even a self-esteem enhancement device — a skeptical empiricist.

Complement The Black Swan, which is fascinating it its totality, with astrophysicist Marcelo Gleiser on how to live with mystery in a culture obsessed with certitude, philosopher Hannah Arendt on how unanswerable questions give shape to the human experience, and novelist Marilynne Robinson on the beauty of the unknown.

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

Before Dark Souls there was King's Field, and five hours into brilliant homage Queen's Domain, it's already one of my favourite games this year

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The clue, of course, is right there in the title. Developer Freshly Baked Games' Queen's Domain isn't just a retro-fantasy action RPG played in first-person; it's an explicit homage to King's Field, the long-dormant FromSoftware series that - as the precursor to Demon's Souls, Dark Souls, Bloodborne, Sekiro, Elden Ring, and all those other Soulslikes - helped shape the modern gaming landscape in its own small way. That, though, is pretty much where my knowledge of the series begins and ends - as much as I love FromSoftware, I've never actually played a King's Field game. I've no idea how Queen's Domain shapes up as Kings Field-like, then, but I do know that, five or so hours in, it's already one of my favourite games this year.

Queen's Domain begins, as many great games do, on a beach. Our hero, Prince Beriik, has been washed ashore after his expedition ship, on a voyage to the mysterious Isle of Neasied, is destroyed. Beriik is searching for his father, King Gius Abraon, who's vanished on a mission to save the kingdom of Bamaen - after years of peace, the evil Queen Siraatsu's army of the damned is stirring; the dead are rising, legendary beasts are returning, and the creatures of the land have turned feral. But for now, there's simply a beach, the cry of distant gulls, and the gentle breaking of surf upon the shore. Queen's Domain's lore might read like an explosion in a vowel factory, but you certainly can't fault its mood.

There's something, perhaps, of the superb Dread Delusion in Queen's Domain; specifically in the way both deploy the lo-fi video game aesthetics of the 90s and early 2000s to invoke a more deliberate kind mood: those unnatural low-poly angles, those shimmering lo-res textures, and those rasterised skies creating - when viewed en masse across an expansive interconnected world - a particularly woozy sort of landscape. Here, though, the vibe isn't so much putrescence as chilly isolation, the muted vistas awash in greens, greys, browns, and blues.

It's a mood sent further off-kilter by the wonderfully odd soundtrack, whose seeping retro synths shift unpredictably from ambient melancholy to thumping dissonance, drawing in everything from parping trumpets to quacking ducks. And then there's the geographic strangeness - the secretly labyrinthine shape of this deceptively compact island - that only grows more apparent the further you explore.

Once you pick yourself off the beach and wander inland, the path almost immediately forks: to the left a boarded trail, to the right a vast stone door. Up ahead a gated cave mouth, and looming high on the clifftop, a mysterious ancient tower. Queen's Domain is full of these kinds of curiosity piquing landmarks, serving as both orientation points and unspoken goals.

Queen's Domain isn't exactly what you'd call forthcoming, you see, and early on - in lieu of a more explicit objective - that tower seems like a promising lead. Rusty blade in hand, you plot a likely course, but while there's a sort of logic to the island from afar, the routes between its landmarks are woven in wonderfully convoluted ways. And before you know it, your path has spilled out onto the coast, winding a perilous cliffside route high above the crashing waves. A second option leads further inland, plunging downward into a shadowy glade. And it's only after battling an onslaught of ladybird-rat hybrids and unnerving chicken-crickets (Queen's Domain loves its weird wildlife amalgams) you realise the two routes converge.

From there, more branches, but always a tangled path; the island's deliberately disorientating design turning navigation and exploration into a fascinating puzzle. And that only increases once you find your first proper sword. Imbued with a powerful darting slash, it's both an offensive tool and - with a break-glass-in-case-of-emergency strafe - an occasionally defensive one. But, it also grants traversal powers, those darting thrusts carrying you across previously impassable gaps. Soon, exploration, initially restricted to ground level, grows increasingly vertiginous, opening up whole new possibilities. You'll bound across treetops, charting a circuitous path around the island, or return to those shattered bridges and see what lies beyond. And that 'what' has frequently managed to surprise me - at one point, an apparently simple ascent turns into a several-hour trawl through a water temple.

Paths aren't always obvious, either; you'll plunge into a pool of deadly miasma, shuttling forward at the last moment to propel yourself along a carefully concealed pipe, or investigate a previously unreachable overhang only to unearth a secret passageway leading to horrifying depths. And often, you'll stumble across sudden shortcuts, causing routes to unexpectedly fold back on themselves. Truthfully, the freedom is mostly an illusion - the island is carefully designed to prevent you from deviating too far - but the proliferation of secret corners, and the minimal handholding, means the paths you're forging feel like your own. And as you unlock new tools, you realise there's a Metroidvania aspect to these spaces too. It's clever stuff, particularly when more overt puzzles are introduced, feeding further into the traversal rhythms.

And throughout it all, combat serves as Queen's Domain's drumbeat, punctuating progress with encounters whose wilfully rudimentary systems do, admittedly, take a bit of getting used to. It's not just the deliberate wooliness of it all as Freshly Baked emulates the combat of video games yore, or enemies' merciless in-your-face lock-on once disturbed, it's the limited ways you can respond. You can swing your sword, step outside of an incoming attack (there's nothing so advanced as a dedicated dodge button), and, well, that's pretty much it. And even accounting for enemies' minimal, largely predictable attack patterns, you'll invariably fumble and die.

But slowly it evolves into something compelling; a methodical game of feints and opportunistic returns, of goading enemies into attacks so you can, as far as your stamina gauge allows, exploit the resulting window. It's still far baggier than, say, Dark Souls, but it finds an engaging rhythm. Partly that's down to a combat twist that actively encourages restraint: standard weapon swings barely do more than chip damage, but hold the button to charge up your attack, and you'll do substantially more harm. That turns combat into a dance of patient timing, as you try and find the perfect moment to unleash a swing as multiple enemies move in.

That's just the foundation though. Soon enough, you've got throwing knives (which slowly replenish over time) for kiting, supplementary damage, or picking off sentry-like foes at a distance. There are spears and bludgeons, often with unusual secondary abilities, and it all feeds into a rock-paper-scissors-style damage system that encourages experimentation with your arsenal - especially where bosses are concerned. And the final flourish is an automatic levelling system that doesn't just strengthen your character as you play, it levels individual weapons - eventually causing them to evolve. It encourages engagement with enemies rather than avoidance, and provides a regular sense of progress in a game where the sometimes unforgiving challenge means inevitable repetition.

Queen's Domain is not, I should stress, a Soulslike, despite some similarities. You don't drop stuff on death, meaning no desperate corpse runs, and while resting and replenishing your energy at crystals resets the world - encouraging some push-your-luck risk-taking - you can save your game independently, creating some welcome breathing room.

Put all this together and Queen's Domain is a wonderful surprise; a moody, sometimes silly retro throwback that, despite its self-imposed mechanical and aesthetic limitations, feels anything but limited. Five hours or so into its supposedly 40 hours of playtime, its mix of combat, exploration, and puzzling has settled into a properly compelling rhythm - and despite some tricky bits and my own cack-handed frustrations, it keeps pulling me back in again. I've no idea if Queen's Domain is a good King's Field homage, but so far at least, it's a bloody good time.

Nintendo Switch 2, PC, PS5, Xbox Series X/S

6 default Pixel 11 settings I changed that made a big difference – and why

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

If you’re a new Pixel 11 user, here are six things I recommend changing right away to get the most from your phone.

I’ve been a dedicated Pixel user since the beginning, so I was naturally excited to get my hands on Google’s latest iteration, the Pixel 11 Pro XL. I’ve been impressed with it overall, but I noticed that, as Google has done before, the device isn’t set up perfectly out of the box.

Also: Pixel 11 is $200 off in this early Black Friday deal

After spending a few weeks with the device, I found that several features are either turned off by default or need optimizing to work in the best way possible.

If you’re a new Pixel 11 user, here are six things I recommend changing right away to get the most from your phone.

The camera was the biggest reason I first joined team Pixel back in 2016, so it’s one of the first places I looked on the Pixel 11 Pro XL. New this year is Camera Looks, a setting that changes how images will be processed before you take them. You can change a number of settings, including contrast, shadows, tint, sharpness, and warmth.

Also: A week with the Pixel 11 changed my mind about Gemini

I’ve always loved the camera on any Pixel I’ve had, but I’ll admit that images can look a little over-processed at times. If picture quality is important to you at all, it’s vital to set this feature right out of the box because it applies to every image you take from that point on. You can find Camera Look by opening the camera app, tapping the settings icon in the bottom-right corner, and making your changes.

Pixel’s “At a Glance” lock screen widgets are one of the most sneakily useful features, and you’re missing out if you’re not using them. These widgets are off by default, but when you enable them, you can see real-time information without unlocking your phone. You can see weather info, sports updates, flight information, rideshare and food delivery updates, fitness information, and more.

Also: These tech gifts topped our experts’ 2026 holiday wish lists

To use these widgets, long-press on an open space on your home screen. To customize your widgets, go to Settings > Wallpaper & style > More lock screen settings > Widgets on lock screen. To access your widgets, you just swipe left from your lock screen.

Out of the box, your Pixel 11’s display isn’t set to utilize its full capability. The Pixel 11 series comes with a fantastic low-temperature polycrystalline oxide (LTPO) display, but it’s slightly throttled in the name of battery life. You’ll notice the increased resolution most on text and in fine details. It won’t make a huge difference to your streaming or large display elements, though. If you read consistently, either articles or e-books, you’ll want to make the change.

To change this, head to Settings > Display > Screen Resolution. Bump the setting from “High resolution,” which is 1080 x 2404, to “Max resolution,” which is 1344 x 2992.

If you’re anything like me, your phone usually needs a quick charge some evenings. The Pixel 11 series introduced Extreme Charging Mode, which accelerates charging by pausing background activity and non-essential apps. Google says the mode should charge the Pixel 11 and 11 Pro to over 50% in 30 minutes, and the Pixel 11 Pro XL to 75% in 30 minutes. From my testing, that estimate was pretty accurate.

Also: After testing the Pixel 11, I feel fine about skipping the Pro models

There are a few caveats: this feature is only available when your phone is at or below 50% battery, and you do need to turn it on every time you use it. But after the first time you do it, it’s available in your quick settings. Head to Settings > Battery & charging > Extreme Charging to turn it on the first time.

If you have the newest Pixel, you might as well take advantage of one of its biggest selling points. I felt like this feature was gimmicky at first (and I’m still a little grumpy it replaced my beloved thermometer), but the more I use it, the more I like it. The multicolor LED on the phone’s rear camera bump can indicate who’s calling, serve as a status indicator for Gemini, and more — and it’s almost certain that more usability will arrive over time.

Where I found the most use was when I had my phone on silent. After assigning specific colors to contacts (you can do this from the contact information screen), I could easily see whether a call was worth picking up without reaching for my phone.

To make sure you’re getting the most out of your new device, head in and change your contact HiLight colors right away. It only takes a few seconds and makes deciding whether to answer a call a lot easier.

We’ve all accidentally swiped away an important notification or missed a notification in a sea of updates. The Pixel 11 offers two notification features to help you stay on top of things.

Notification History is a feature that logs every alert from the last 24 hours, including those you swipe away. This is off by default, meaning when you dismiss a notification, it’s gone forever. With this on, you get a searchable, chronological backup.

Also: Google’s big flagship is more Pro than ever

AI Notification Summaries, as the name implies, takes long multi-notification threads and stacked notifications and turns them into bullet points. This feature is powered by on-device Gemini Intelligence, and you can choose which apps to summarize.

You can find both of these under Settings > Notifications > Notification history & Notification Summaries.

Bitcoin ETF Attracts $75M as Goldman Sachs Calls December Fed Rate Cut

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Ahmed Barakat is a journalist and copywriter based in Georgia with a growing focus on blockchain technology, DeFi, AI, privacy, digital assets, and fintech innovation.

The CryptoNews editorial team is composed of seasoned writers specializing in cryptocurrency and blockchain technology. Their expertise ensures comprehensive, accurate, and insightful content for...

Bitcoin ETF demand returned on November 4 as Bitcoin recovered above $81,000, but Wall Street’s outlook for Federal Reserve rate cuts remains uncertain. Goldman Sachs pushed its expected Fed rate cuts back by one quarter, citing persistent inflation and a labor market that remains resilient. The bank now expects reductions in December 2026 and March 2027.

U.S. spot Bitcoin ETF recorded $74.6 million in net inflows, ending three consecutive sessions of withdrawals totaling $1.16 billion. Fidelity’s FBTC led the recovery, while BlackRock’s IBIT continued losing capital.

Fidelity’s Wise Origin Bitcoin Fund attracted approximately $236.7 million on November 4. Bitwise’s BITB added $15.2 million, while ARK Invest’s ARKB received $19.9 million.

BlackRock’s iShares Bitcoin Trust moved in the opposite direction, recording $201.2 million in outflows. The withdrawals extended IBIT’s four-session losing streak to $935 million. These mixed flows show that institutional demand has not returned uniformly across the market.

Bitcoin also recovered from a seven-week low near $75,600 during the November 3 election period, climbing back above $81,000 today. The rebound followed market stabilization after the U.S. midterm elections, although analysts cautioned against attributing the recovery to a single catalyst.

Discover: The Crypto Presales This Q4 2026

Goldman Sachs now expects the Federal Reserve to deliver its next two rate cuts in December 2026 and March 2027, pushing both reductions back by one quarter.

The revised forecast shows concerns that energy costs could keep core personal consumption expenditures inflation near 3% throughout the year. Goldman economist David Mericle said lower inflation readings and further labor market softening would likely be necessary for the Fed to cut rates before year-end.

The bank maintained its projected terminal rate range of 3% to 3.25%, while acknowledging that the Fed could decide against additional cuts if economic conditions remain strong.

Bank of America adopted an even more cautious outlook, removing its expected 2026 rate cuts and moving them into the third quarter of 2027. This shift creates a more challenging macro backdrop for Bitcoin, which often benefits when borrowing costs fall and liquidity conditions improve.

But the latest Bitcoin ETF inflows suggest some investors are still willing to increase exposure despite the delayed easing outlook. The contrast between returning fund demand and a more cautious Fed forecast gives Bitcoin traders two competing signals.

Institutional buying could help support prices if inflows continue, while elevated interest rates may limit the liquidity available for risk assets.

The Best Fish Finders of 2026, Expert Tested

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn more

Love it or hate it, marine electronics are a big part of modern bass fishing. In recent years, an underwater arms race has engulfed the sport, with manufacturers competing to top each other with the newest tech. From mapping to side sonar to live sonar, anglers can combine countless options on the front and back of a boat to help locate fish.

Since Humminbird, Lowrance, and Garmin are the frontrunners in the freshwater market, we reached out to all three and asked them to send a graph that supports their most advanced features and hardware. Then we installed each system on a boat and put them to the test in a side-by-side comparison to find the best mapping, forward-facing sonar, and side sonar. Here are the results.

The Garmin LiveScope outperformed both Lowrance and Humminbird’s forward-facing sonar technologies. I tested Garmin’s LiveScope system on their 106SV EchoMAP Ultra 2 10-inch screen unit. On the water, I set the forward-facing sonar (FFS) gains/contrasts to “Auto.” Then I set the unit to read 70 feet out and 20 feet down. While this range is a little less than I would typically use when running a single unit, I chose 70 feet instead of 100 so I could get a closer look at the fish, bait, structure, and cover to compare. Ultimately, I found my eyes gravitating back to the Garmin screen time and time again during the side-by-side test.

The image was a smidge clearer on the Garmin than on the Humminbird, and both were superior to the image I got on the Lowrance. The difference came down to bottom separation and target definition at greater distances. Past 30 feet, the detail was clearer on the Garmin. You can also pick up your bait better on the LiveScope Plus System.

Garmin was first to market with live sonar, and the rest of the industry is still playing catch-up. Their LiveScope is the best at identifying species and baitfish in real time, as well as surveying structure and cover. This is as close as you can get to a live-streaming camera beneath the surface that can reveal things at great distances, making the Garmin the best choice for targeting suspended fish in open water and picking apart submerged cover like hydrilla and brush.

The only place the Garmin paled was installation. You must install an additional black box with the Garmin LVS34 transducer. However, Garmin just released two new transducers that no longer require a black box, which tips the scales even more in Garmin’s favor.  I tested the system on Garmin’s 106SV EchoMAP Ultra 2 with a 10-inch screen. This model also comes in 12- and 16-inch sizes, and a half dozen other units are compatible with the LiveScope system.

To evaluate side sonar, I set each graph to read 50 feet left and 50 feet right, then compared the images. All three transducers marked the same cover, and each returned good detail. However, Lowrance’s side imaging edged out the other units. When talking about “more detail,” we’re really splitting hairs. When scanning by a tree, for example, it can come down to counting more limbs on one screen than the other. All three transducers collected and conveyed a ton of data; the Lowrance just did a little bit more.

Installing this unit and transducer was fairly easy, and I was impressed with the operating system on the Lowrance HDS Pro 10. This unit had a 10-inch screen that was ultra-clear and easy to read, even in bright conditions. Navigation through the different screens and settings was intuitive. The HDS Pro also comes in 12- and 16-inch screen sizes. If you want this graph for SideScan and a different unit for FFS, the 10 or 12 is sufficient. This technology is better for finding a productive area while idling, then switching to FFS to pick cover apart and catch fish.

Depending on your area, certain maps will be better than others simply because one company has better coverage than another. For me here in Alabama, Humminbird’s LakeMaster charts outpaced Lowrance’s C-Map and Garmin’s Navionics. Over the years, I have found that each of these products is on certain lakes. Some are more detailed, while others map lesser-known lakes that another company hasn’t mapped yet. So the best mapping is really subjective to where you’ll be doing most of your fishing.

What sets Humminbird’s LakeMaster charts apart is their coverage of my area. While other brands also offer customizable depth shading and identify road beds, sunken bridges, channel markers, and other features, having better maps on the lakes I visit most often put the LakeMaster charts at the top of the heap. You can do a lot with the Minn Kota LakeMaster VX Premium Digital Charts, from 1-foot contour lines to custom shading and even satellite image overlay directly onto the maps. The latter can be really helpful when trying to find isolated backwaters that don’t appear on traditional contour maps, as well as shallow brush and rock piles on lakes with wintertime drawdowns—since this cover may appear as dry land depending on when the satellite images were taken.

Of these different technologies, mapping units are the easiest to install. You can connect a transducer to the same unit or an external GPS puck, but the graph has an internal GPS antenna, so you don’t need any additional hardware beyond what comes with the graph. This means you can connect power to the graph and immediately pull up the maps, your position on the maps, and start plotting. Do some research to figure out what maps cover your area best, but for me, Humminbird’s LakeMaster charts were the best.

Humminbird’s Mega Live 2 was extremely sensitive and could detect baits at long distances, and it marked the same cover, baitfish, and larger fish as the Garmin unit.  I like that Humminbird’s Mega Live 2 doesn’t require an external black box or module, unlike the Lowrance ActiveTarget 2 XL and Garmin’s LVS34. (Note: Garmin’s next-gen transducers no longer require a black box.) The lack of a black box made the Humminbird significantly easier to install, and the lack of additional hardware likely contributes to the lower price of Humminbird’s Mega Live 2 compared to Lowrance’s and Garmin’s offerings, which both cost an additional $200.

I had issues getting Mega Live 2 to read on the last couple of outings. I’m troubleshooting that now, but this technical glitch is the only drawback I’ve experienced with Humminbird’s FFS so far. While the 10-inch Xplore unit I tested with the Mega Live 2 was compatible in size with the other brands, it couldn’t really maximize the full potential of the Mega Live 2 system. If you want to use this as your FFS, consider upgrading to a larger 12- or 16-inch screen.

And when we write about a product—whether it’s a bass lure or a backpack—we cover the good and the bad, so you know exactly what to expect before you decide to make a purchase.

Snow Capped Mountain Views in Arizona

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

This morning I got up nice and early at our tent site before the sun came up. The wind was pretty bad last night and was whipping our tents around. I managed to sleep just fine though. Once I got up this morning I made coffee and then began breaking down camp. Then we got moving just as the sun was coming up.

The goal for today is basically just to hike as far as we possibly can. I was looking through my food last night to access how much I had left. There are a couple of different options in terms of which road we hitch into to get back to Flagstaff. If we push really hard we could get in tomorrow night. And if we go a little slower or go further we could get in Sunday night. I think even being out for 2 more full days is going to be cutting it close in terms of how much food I have left. I definitely did grab enough from Gorf’s car. Or maybe I’ve just been eating more than I expected to. Resupplying is always one of the hardest things to do on a thru hike in my opinion. I’ve thru hiked nearly 23,000 miles and I still struggle with it regularly. It can be hard to properly predict the kind of appetite you’ll have and also what kinds of foods will be appealing to you.

Our day started with some double track dirt road walking, with Mount Humphreys peaking out in the distance.

Once we got going the trail continued through rolling fields of shrubs and small trees. It reminds me of a Montana prairie at times to be honest. I’m surprised how the Arizona Trail can remind me of so many different places all at once! Some sections remind me of Montana, Idaho, Wyoming, and even Colorado and New Mexico. Our route brought us through the Babbitt ranch passage this morning. I believe we’ll continue on this passage through the ranch property for the next full day at least.

Unfortunately the wind that persisted for the entire day yesterday was still raging on today. It was still only around 20-30 MPH with gusts up to 40 MPH. There are certainly worse environmental and weather conditions that you can deal with on the trail other than wind, but it can still be its own problem. There have been a handful of days on the trail over the years where I encountered wind around 50-60 MPH consistently. That speed of wind can literally push you over at times. I always try to remember those moments when I get bothered by some light, manageable wind.

The miles were nice and easy this morning through this prairie.

As we continued on this morning the trail weaved through wide grasslands with views of Mount Humphreys off in the distance. We’re going to get closer and closer to the mountain over the course of the day until we eventually go right by the base of it. There is a side trail to bag the peak which is around 13,500 feet high. It was absolutely stunning watching the mountain get closer and closer as the morning progressed. The peak got snow in the recent rainstorm, and it looks lovely up there now.

Early on in the morning I had already begun to feel a pretty ravenous hunger coursing through me. I don’t know if it’s because I’m aware of how little food I have, or if it’s just true hiker hunger setting in. Either way, I was starving. I ate a bunch of my snacks and the remaining rations for the whole day were already running low. Every now and then Slide would snack on something and offer me some of it, knowing I really didn’t have much. I’ll have plenty to eat to get to the next town safely. But when you’re hungry out here sometimes it is really hard to satisfy with just small snacks and things. I call it a hunger that only town food can solve.

As the morning went on we got closer and closer to the massive mountain in the distance.

Around 10:30AM we went past signs for a wildlife watering station, which is of course our next water source. We’re the wildlife out here now! It was just off the trail and a necessary stop. The next sources all look pretty nasty, and the most appealing next source is a long way ahead. We won’t reach that until sometime this evening around the time it gets dark. The wind was still whipping by the time we arrived so we hid behind some shrubs for a brief reprieve. I appreciate the brief “quiet” as well.

After we left the water source we continued along similar terrain for the next few hours. The trail got a bit more “forested” at times, especially in terms of a desert environment. We even watched a large group of elk which were grazing on a hill above the trail. There was one big bull in the group and they all scattered when they heard us coming from a distance. Elk hunting season just started here and the elk definitely know it. We’ve seen a lot of hunters around.

This puffy plant looks like some sort of milkweed. It looked wonderful dancing in the wind.

For most of the afternoon we were continuing through pastures and grasslands, with Mount Humphreys getting progressively closer. The wind made it comfortable to keep all of my layers on for most of the day. I quite enjoy hiking a bit more bundled up when it’s comfortable to do so. It feels cozy! I can’t believe my Sambob Alpha 90 layers are still going out here on the AZT after all of the miles in them last year. But they sure are. And I’ve been loving the ibex wool air puffy that I’m using out on a longer thru hike for the first time. That thing is really cozy but doesn’t get me too sweaty because of the wool. I’m surprised how much I’ve hiked in it on the AZT. I think I’ve done more hiking in a puffy on this trail than any other!

Later on in the afternoon the trail transitioned a bit from the never-ending grasslands to a taller wheatgrass. Which might not seem like anything too special, but is incredibly beautiful while it blows in the wind. All of the tall grass and wildflowers were dancing around in the heavy wind. That was the one bright side of the wind I suppose. By this time Mount Humphreys and its snow-covered peak were right in front of us as well. Boy, is that mountain massive and stunning. It’s hard to imagine such a large peak in Arizona.

Mount Humphreys much closer in the distance now!

Slide was up ahead of me at one point and stopped off to look at something alongside the trail. It was a horny toed lizard that was absolutely adorable. It stuck around for a while and we got to get really close to it. That was one of the bigger horny toed lizards that I’ve seen.

In the late afternoon the sun actually peaked out from the clouds for the first time all day. I was still wearing my Alpha and my puffy and all of a sudden it was scorching out! And just as quickly the clouds rolled back in and I was cold again with fewer layers on. The desert is a wild place.

The trail continued to bless us with great views, large juniper trees, and flowers. As well as those lovely little bridges across the cattle guards. There were some large white plants along the trail which looked like a sort of fluffy milkweed, and those looked fantastic in the blowing wind.

By around 4:30PM the trail had brought us out onto dirt roads. We passed by a bike packer who stopped to chat with us for a minute. He said the upcoming water source was fantastic, which is always nice to hear. But to get to the water source we would have to climb up a particularly steep hill. There was no choice but to climb it. Up top there was a massive trough overflowing with cold, clear water. The recent rain had that thing filled to the brim! As we sat there and the wind blew, water splashed up over the edge of the trough. Slide and I sat there and filled up everything that we would need for the rest of the day, overnight, and tomorrow morning.

Our delicious water source for the evening. It was ice cold and crystal clear thanks to lots of rain.

When we left the water source we got to enjoy going back down that large hill. Then continued along a dirt road for the remainder of the night. By 5PM we had already made it 28 miles, but still had a ways to go for the day. I’m always pleased though when the mileage remaining for the day is less than 10 miles. Somehow 9 miles or less feels very manageable. It’s easy to tell myself that I only have a few more hours of hiking to go.

Before the sun set a jackrabbit ran by us near the trail and we got to watch it scamper off. That was delightful. Those jackrabbits are so fast and hear you coming from a mile away! Then we enjoyed glorious views of the sunset over a field, with Mount Humphreys off in the distance. And a lone cow was out there in the field too in front of the mountain. I thought that made the moment even more beautiful.

This cow looked like it was modeling for calendar or something in front of Mount Humphreys.

My favorite time of day always seems to be when the sun is going down. Watching the sunset is a reward for a long day of hiking. Hiking into the dark is a fair price to pay to get to bear witness to the totality of a glorious sunset. By the time the sun went down I was quite ready to be done for the day. I’d been tired for a while but managed to keep it moving regardless.

Sometime after 7PM I checked my watch and saw we had gone about 36 miles for the day. So we continued along and started looking for a nice flat spot to get set up for the night. We wound up going 36.8 miles before finding a spot and calling it a day. I was tired but felt good! My calves were still sore today, but not any worse than yesterday. They were maybe ever so slightly less sore today. What more could you ask for? After I got my tent set up I made dinner quickly and did my stretching. Then I hardly had any energy left and just called it a night.

If you enjoyed this blog and would like to support my hike by buying me a soda or a snack along the way, there is a “Tip the Author” button below! It links directly to my Venmo. Any and all support is incredibly appreciated!

The buyer pays the same price as they would otherwise, and your purchase helps to support The Trek's ongoing goal to serve you quality backpacking advice and information. Thanks for your support!

I’m a 29 year old thru hiker who has walked over 22,000 miles over the past handful of years. In 2017 I attempted my first thru hike ever, out on the PCT. I completed 1,300~ miles in between semesters of college. Then went back in 2022 and hiked the entire trail over again. The following year in 2023 I hiked the entire 5,600 mile eastern continental trail. Which stretches from Key West, Florida all the way to Newfoundland, Canada. When I completed that trek, I became the 2nd woman in history to hike the entire trail. In 2024 I completed my thru hiking triple crown, after completing the CDT. Then in 2025 I became the 1st woman in history to complete the "border to border" calendar year triple crown. When I finished, I decided to continue hiking for the rest of the year, completing 10,070~ miles in 2025. More miles than any other woman has ever hiked in 1 year and just shy of the men's over-all mileage record as well.

Exclusive: Matt Jarvis backs ‘exceptional’ Scott Parker in verdict on West Ham’s Nuno sacking

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

West Ham parted ways with Nuno Espirito Santo on Tuesday, and swiftly named Scott Parker as his replacement. The new boss led WHUFC for Wednesday’s 3-3 draw at Cardiff City, and many are backing him for success.

Among the supporters of Parker is former Hammers winger Matt Jarvis, who believes inconsistency ultimately cost Nuno his job. Speaking to The72 via Midnite, Jarvis said: “The fans’ perspective flipped from last year’s misery to expecting to dominate this league and win promotion. Visiting the London Stadium in front of 55,000 to 60,000 supporters is every opposition team’s cup final, and adapting to that takes time.

“With Nuno, people forget he had been there for a significant period. It took him time to establish himself in the Premier League, then they had a good spell before fading towards the end. The board backed him in the transfer market after relegation, but results remained too inconsistent. The supporters couldn’t see a clear playing identity, and the home defeat to West Brom proved the final straw.”

Jarvis went on to talk up Parker’s chances of success as West Ham boss. He picked out his communication skills, experience and passion as key traits as he looks to take the Hammers back to the Premier League.

“People will debate whether the decision came too early or whether they should have waited for the international break, but the club felt a change was needed. They have four upcoming fixtures where they should realistically target three points from every game.

“Scott Parker has a fantastic Championship track record and an existing connection to the club.

“He’s an exceptional communicator, which is vital for translating training-ground instruction onto the pitch on matchdays. He’s passionate, knows what it takes to get out of this division, and his arrival should have an immediate impact.”

Parker has won three promotions from the Championship, achieving the feat at Fulham, Bournemouth and Burnley. In his most recent triumph, he led the Clarets to 2nd place in 2024/25 and broke the league record for the fewest goals conceded in an entire campaign at just 16.

The switch from Nuno to Parker has split some opinions. There are justified concerns that their play styles are too similar given the new boss’ recent teams have been built on the foundation of a strong defence.

This was the case in his last job at Burnley, but the 46-year-old has shown he has the ability to switch up his approach. At Fulham in the 2020/21 season, his side had the second-highest average possession in the Championship with 61.4% (FotMob), as well 85 big chances created and 1,101 touches in the opposition box.

Over time, it will become clear whether Parker plans on emulating his Burnley tactics at WHUFC, or his Fulham style. Alternatively, he could switch things up completely, and if he does, ex-Hammer Jarvis believes he has the know-how and communication skills to get this across to his new squad.

After taking charge of the Cardiff City draw a matter of hours after his appointment on Wednesday, Parker leads his side for the second time against Blackburn Rovers this Saturday.

Midnite, the Principal Partner of Wolverhampton Wanderers for the 2026/27 season, are providing luxury coach travel on the “Midnite Express” for 100 Wolverhampton Wanderers supporters, as they set off on a 130-mile-round trip to watch their beloved Wolves take on the league’s glamour club, Wrexham, this Saturday, November 7.

Fans will be joined on the Express by Wolves club legends, Steve Bull, Carl Ikeme and Matt Jarvis and treated to club-centric quizzes and live Q&As, with exclusive prizes up for grabs.

Dragon Pose Is the Essential Recovery Stretch For Tight Hips and Back

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

The yin yoga pose we love to hate, Dragon Pose, is a smart recovery tool for cyclists to improve hip extension. Because cycling involves repetitive hip flexion at the top of the pedal stroke, the primary hip flexor muscle (psoas major) can tighten and become compressed over time, making it harder to access a powerful position off the bike.

The low torso angle on a tri-specific bike provides aerodynamic benefits, but also requires greater hip flexion. In the aero position, the hip flexors, which not only flex the hip but also stabilize the pelvis and lower back, repeatedly contract without fully extending, shortening the muscles and potentially leading to low back pain and stiffness.

Dragon Pose is useful for triathletes who spend a lot of time folded over the handlebars during indoor training through the winter. It can also help you during the warmer months by restoring hip extension and preparing you for the longer stride that running off the bike demands. On the run, you repeatedly load one leg as the hip extends behind the body. Dragon Pose simulates that position, so you step off the bike feeling ready to hit your running stride.

Add Dragon Pose to your recovery routine. Practice this pose after a training ride or race to stretch the hip flexors and improve hip mobility.

Keep in mind that multiple bike fit factors affect hip angle and comfort. This guide provides five elements of a proper bike fit for smoother pedaling and to keep your position within your comfortable hip flexion range.

Improving hip extension can help you feel less compressed on the bike, move more easily through transitions, and ease low-back discomfort.

Best Ammo for Deer Hunting, Tested for Accuracy

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 5, 2026 1:02 PM EST

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

The latest for gun hunters and competitive shooters.

Every major ammunition company has a specific product line dedicated to whitetail hunters. Ideally, this ammo will provide good accuracy, function reliably, be widely available, and deliver consistent terminal performance – all at a wallet-friendly price.

I tested six popular whitetail loads to compare their precision at the bench, how they fed and cycled, and the consistency of their velocities in an effort to determine which shot best and whether any should be avoided. This video shows all the results in real time and gives details on their relative performance.

All the loads were chambered in 6.5 Creedmoor. I chose the 6.5 Creed because it is today’s most popular big-game centerfire rifle cartridge; it is stocked everywhere from Alabama to Alaska; and, as the poster child of Modern Cartridge Design, it is inherently accurate and I wanted the ammo in this test to perform as well as possible.

I put each load through two different rifles. One is the Seekins SIC Short Action, a high-end precision rifle that I’ve been using extensively for the last year and is slated to be released to the public in 2027. The other is a Ruger American Rifle Gen II, one of the best all-around hunting rifles for those on a budget. We’ve been big fans of the Ruger American since it was introduced – it won an Editor’s Choice in our annual gun test when it debuted – and the platform continues to impress us with its combination of accuracy-enhancing features, good stock ergonomics, and smooth-cycling action.

I put 20 rounds of each whitetail ammo through each rifle for a total of 40 rounds per offering. I measured those 5-shot groups on my E.A.T.S. targets to come up with an average group size by rifle and then calculated the combined average group size of all eight groups through both rifles.

I captured velocity data on a Garmin Xero C2 Chronograph. The Garmin’s ability to stop and restart sessions allowed me to gather 20 shots of data with each gun and ammo combo for the velocity averages, extreme spreads (ES), and standard deviations (SD). With factory ammo, any 20-shot SD in the teens is very good and there’s nothing wrong with values in the 20s either, especially for the center-of-mass deer hunting ammo we’re showcasing here.

The results of the test were fascinating. The Winchester Deer Season XP and Barnes Harvest performed extremely well, Federal’s Fusion Tipped delivered very good precision, the Nosler Whitetail Country and Hornady American Whitetail loads were solid all around, and the Remington Core-Lokt Tipped gave mixed results that serve as a cautionary tale when selecting hunting ammo.

This data is based on the average of four 5-shot groups with each rifle and ammo combination. The combined value is the average of how the ammunition did in both the Seekins SIC SA and the Ruger American Rifle Gen II.

The muzzle velocity and standard deviation data is also based on 20 shots, except for the Federal 140-grain Fusion Tipped, which is based on 30 shots of data.

This is the most visually distinctive ammo in the test with Winchester’s oversized polymer tip that’s designed to produce violent and quick bullet upset upon impact.

This ammunition delivered the best groups overall, with a 5-shot average of .700 inches. The 20 shots through the Seekins averaged .652 inches, while the Ruger’s groups averaged .749 inches.

You can find it online for about $35 for a box of 20, but it is currently hard to find a vendor that has it in stock. The 125-grain version is available but wasn’t the load we tested so we can’t assume it will perform at the same level.

The Harvest Collection is the love child that resulted from the union of Barnes Bullets and Sierra Bullets, which were joined in corporate matrimony back in 2020. I’ve had excellent results with this ammunition, which was officially unveiled in April 2025, both in terms of its accuracy at the bench and its performance in the field.

The idea behind the Harvest Collection is simple. Barnes took Sierra’s well-regarded Tipped Game King bullet and loaded it in their brass on their machinery, which has a reputation for holding tight tolerances.

With an overall average 5-shot group size of .704 inches, it finished just behind Winchester in this evaluation. That said, I’ve had even better results with this ammunition in other rifles, such as my Sako TRG 22-A1. My groups through that rifle averaged .491 inches at 2692 fps with a 15.7 fps SD.

I’ve used this load on a few whitetails at this point, and the terminal results have been excellent too.

You can find the 6.5 Creedmoor load online for about $40 per box of 20.

The Fusion Tipped bullet has become a go-to of mine in the last couple of years not only for whitetails, but also antelope, elk and mule deer.

In terms of its construction, it falls between traditional cup-and-core projectiles and true bonded bullets with respect to its toughness. It’ll retain weight better than a non-bonded bullet but is cheaper to produce than a chemically bonded bullet and has the added benefit of being more inherently accurate than one, too.

In truth, the Fusion Tipped strikes a beautiful balance between accuracy, terminal performance, and economy. During this test the 140-grain Fusion Tipped averaged .989 inches in group size, with the Seekins turning in .784-inch groups and the Ruger delivering 1.194-inch groups.

I’ve found the 6.5 Creedmoor online for $46 to $50 per box, which makes it one of the more expensive whitetail-oriented ammo offerings. If that’s a bit rich for your blood, consider the traditional Fusion line, which can usually be found for a few dollars less per box.

The Nosler Solid Base bullet was first introduced more than half a century ago, but it never caught on like the company’s iconic Partition and Ballistic Tipped projectiles and there were years where the company halted production altogether.

But this cup-and-core soft point, with its tapered jacket, makes for a dandy hunting bullet for whitetails and other big game at moderate distances and it is enjoying a revival of sorts in Nosler’s Whitetail Country lineup.

The bullet’s accuracy in this test was solid. With 5-shot groups averaging 1.167 inches it is more than capable of taking deer at distances to 300 yards or more. Interestingly, this ammunition was the only one in the test where the budget-oriented Ruger American Rifle outshot my fancy-pants Seekins SIC. My four 5-shot groups from the Ruger averaged 1.131 inches, besting the Seekins which averaged 1.202 inches.

The 140-grain 6.5 Creed load goes for $33 to $40 per box of 20 online, making it one of the more affordable selections in the test.

Inside the jacket of this bullet is a raised ring that helps keep the lead core in place as it expands and penetrates within the target. That “locking” element gave the InterLock its name and it’s been part of the American hunting landscape for half a century now.

Loaded in the American Whitetail line, it is one of the most affordable bread-and-butter offerings for big-game hunters. The price per box hovers around $33 currently.

Its attractive price tag and its reasonable performance — through the two test rifles its average 5-shot group average measured 1.375 inches — earned it a Great Buy award in this round up.

This ammunition delivered the largest overall groups in the test, with a 5-shot average of 1.519 inches. On the surface, that’s mediocre performance, but it comes with a significant caveat.

The Ruger American Rifle Gen II did not care for this ammunition. The four 5-shot groups measured 2.065, 2.699, 2.392, and 2.143 inches, for an average of 2.325 inches. That isn’t a result I’d feel confident hunting with, and I doubt many of you would be satisfied with it either.

But through the Seekins SIC SA, the ammo hammered. Those four groups came in at 1.039, .595, .589, and .630 inches, averaging .713 inches overall. Across the 12 rifle and ammo combinations in the test that was third only to how well the Seekins shot the Barnes Harvest ammo at .642 inches and Winchester ammo at .652 inches.

The lessons here are that there’s no reason for you to go afield with substandard precision from a modern hunting rifle shooting quality modern ammunition, and that it pays to test out different loads when preparing for firearms season.

The Core-Lokt Tipped offering in 6.5 Creed runs around $43 online currently.

Investing in a few boxes of ammunition is all it takes to help your deer rifle reach its precision potential. Four 5-shot groups will yield more reliable data than three, but even 15 well-placed deliberate shots on target will give you a good feel for the performance of your system.

In any event, there’s no reason to head to your favorite stand or block of public ground with a nagging sense of uncertainty about how your rifle will shoot when that big buck steps into the clear.

As you can see from this evaluation, even with a budget-minded rig like the Ruger, you can have a true sub-MOA rifle if you take the time to figure out what your hunting rig likes.

John B. Snow is Shooting Editor of Outdoor Life, where he oversees the publication’s firearms and shooting coverage. This includes gear reviews, features on technical innovations, stories on shooting techniques and general hunting coverage with the occasional fishing story thrown into the mix.

Ads Drive Consumer Demand. The Shelf Captures It

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Imagine you’re shopping for laundry detergent. You click to buy your preferred brand, but when your order is fulfilled, the product is out of stock. What do you do?

Recent DoorDash data shows that shoppers in this scenario buy a competing brand nearly 12% of the time, while more than 40% of the time they request a refund instead. In either case, the preferred brand just lost an easy opportunity to convert on strong demand.

This isn’t a niche problem. Inventory distortion from out-of-stocks and overstocks costs the retail industry an estimated $1.7 trillion each year, according to IHL Group. That makes capturing that demand a huge growth opportunity for brands.

The challenge here is that most commerce media platforms are great at measuring advertising performance but don’t fully understand the conditions under which advertising is trying to convert. That missing context is becoming increasingly consequential as brands invest more heavily in commerce media.

Commerce media’s greatest strength is proximity to the transaction. Advertisers can connect media exposure to purchases and measure impact against verified sales.

But many other factors can derail conversion. A product might be missing from the retailer’s digital assortment, or the listing might lack a title, image or product detail to give the shopper confidence. In each case, the brand doesn’t have a full view of the lost opportunity.

Conventional advertising metrics can tell us what happened (click/no click, sale/no sale, etc.), but often lack the context to explain why or why not, as the case may be. And if marketers don’t understand why something happened, it’s easy to optimize for the wrong variable.

When a campaign delivers strong engagement but disappointing sales or category share, the natural response is to adjust the media strategy by changing the targeting or creative or shifting spending toward other products or retailers. But what if the campaign was reaching the right consumers and generating genuine purchase intent only to encounter one of the availability problems discussed above?

In that case, shifting budget away from the “underperforming” product or retailer actually means walking away from demonstrated consumer demand.

The same applies to substitution behavior. When shoppers can’t get their preferred product, do they choose another product within the brand’s portfolio, switch to a competitor or abandon the purchase altogether?

Each of those actions merits a distinct strategic response. A product that consistently loses shoppers to competitors during stockouts might warrant greater attention from sales and supply-chain teams. A product with strong demand but poor digital visibility might need better catalog execution to maximize advertising outcomes.

The takeaway is that advertising amplifies demand and content drives conversion – but growth is delivered on the shelf. And without visibility into that full order journey, it’s easy to pour money into fixing a problem that doesn’t exist.

These gaps in contextual understanding stem from a familiar problem in ad tech: fragmented workflows and siloed data. Marketing teams manage campaign strategy, delivery and performance. Ecommerce oversees product listings and content. Sales and category teams manage retailer relationships and assortment. Supply-chain teams focus on inventory and availability.

Organizations haven’t connected the data in a way that helps them understand if and where demand hits a snag along the buying journey. For decades, point of sale and syndicated sales data has helped CPGs understand what sold in stores. But sales data alone rarely explains why a product didn’t sell. Was it out of stock? Was the shopper’s preferred item or size unavailable? Did they choose another product or switch to a competitor when at the shelf?

But even when brands have inventory data, they can’t always keep up with what shoppers encounter. Shelves change by the hour and any data source – whether it’s a brand’s, a retailer’s or a periodic audit – is just a snapshot.

Newer approaches are helping close that gap by connecting advertising data with real-world commerce signals. At DoorDash, for example, we’re evolving Ads Manager into Brand Center, bringing advertising performance together with on-shelf availability, assortment insights and consumer purchasing insights. By combining order data with in-store signals captured by Dashers, brands can see not only where sales are happening, but where demand may be going unfulfilled due to out-of-stocks and what shoppers choose instead when a product isn’t available.

This integrated visibility is helping different teams within an organization work from a shared understanding of what’s constraining growth so they can figure out who can actually do something about it.

Commerce media has rapidly become a core marketing channel because of how it’s made advertising more measurable and more optimizable. But to mature further, commerce media performance needs to be connected with the broader commercial context that determines whether demand becomes revenue.

That deeper contextual visibility brings a new set of questions that lead to critical insights, including, “Which products are generating demand that isn’t being fulfilled?” and “Where are availability or assortment gaps suppressing sales?”

Marketers don’t have to become inventory managers or supply-chain experts. But they should have enough visibility to recognize when a disappointing campaign result isn’t actually an advertising problem. Because a commerce media program with impressive advertising metrics isn’t worth much if it’s not driving real revenue.

Sometimes, the best way to improve advertising performance has nothing to do with the ad itself.

High-end ad blockers mimic some of the same behavior as bot traffic. So WiseGuyGaming repurposed the tools it was already using to identify bots to reveal its ad-blocking audience as well.

Criteo has weathered considerable storms in its day, including a painful moment in March 2020 when its market cap bottomed out below $500 million. Can it pull off yet another turnaround?

Disaffection and outright anger toward US sports institutions run deeper than logo-cluttered fields. Leagues and teams are sacrificing player safety and fan enjoyment for marketing dollars.

People Inc.’s referral traffic from Google search is down 70% over the past two years, and by 26% in Q3 alone. But its digital revenue is still growing, albeit slowly.

On Tuesday, Chord, a six-year-old commerce tech startup, acquired Zenlytic, an AI startup with an anthropomorphized data analyst agent named “Zoë,” for $5 million.

If you ask the executive leadership of Affle and Perion why the two companies are merging, the answer you’ll get is “synergies.”

AdExchanger is where marketers, agencies, publishers and tech companies go for the latest information on the trends that are transforming digital media and marketing, from data, privacy, identity and AI to commerce, CTV, measurement and mobile.

How Triathlon and Incurable Cancer Have Given Me a Way to Live Despite My Diagnosis

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Age-group triathlete Rebecca Jaussi shares her personal story with Triathlete of how receiving a cancer diagnosis drove her to signing up for and racing an Ironman 70.3.

I have spent most of my life making plans.

Endless lists of goals. Race calendars. Career milestones. Bucket-list adventures. I have always believed that if I worked hard enough and planned carefully enough, I might chart the course of my life.

Running and occasional triathlons are at the top of my goal-making lists.

I was a late starter professionally, choosing to stay home with my children when they were young. When I eventually returned to school, I didn’t ease my way back in. I was accepted into an accelerated doctoral nursing program, completing an intense year of coursework and clinicals before becoming a registered nurse, followed by three years of doctoral study. In 2020, I earned my doctorate in nursing practice and became a family nurse practitioner.

I have always been willing to push myself, physically and mentally, and my tenacity has benefited me.

I never imagined I would need it to fight cancer.

This story began with a fall. It was another beautiful morning on the North Shore of Oahu (where I was living at the time) in February 2023, and I was running a technical trail when I went down on a rocky section. I experienced a pop in my right hamstring and some pain. But I did what I always do and just kept going.

I finished the run. I experienced some pain afterward, but not enough, in my mind, to stop running for good. I took a few days off and then returned to the trails.

As a healthcare provider, I knew better. But as a runner, I convinced myself otherwise.

I continued running through an increasingly persistent, insidious pain. Eventually, my pace deteriorated from slow to a limping shuffle. Finally, I went to my colleague who practiced sports medicine, and was referred to physical therapy.

I started PT in May. My physical therapist told me I needed to stop running, and I thought that was an unreasonable course of action. There have been very few occasions in my life when I stopped running, including childbirth and surgery, and they were brief periods of time.

Running wasn’t simply exercise for me; it was where I found my flow state and truly part of my identity.

I didn’t feel ready or willing to give up running due to the pain but trusted my physical therapist, whom I considered a friend and a respected colleague, that this was the best course of action.

For three months, I walked on flat trails. I continued my physical therapy exercises and cross-trained but abstained from running or hiking.

When I returned for another assessment, my physical therapist was puzzled. I wasn’t improving. He suggested imaging, and that push changed everything.

I went in for an MRI of my right leg. The imaging was ordered for my mid-thigh, which was not where I had originally injured my hamstring. When I discussed this with the technician, I pointed out that it was in my hip region, so he modified the imaging order.

The MRI showed enlarged lymph nodes in my right groin, hip, and possibly behind my knee, which was an abnormal finding.

I had no recent infection or other obvious explanation for the swollen lymph nodes. I asked colleagues and peers for their opinions, and most believed this was a benign finding.

My mind vacillated between thinking that it was probably fine and the worst-case scenario of malignancy. It felt unsettling to me, and I decided to push for a biopsy rather than wait a few months for an ultrasound to see if the lymph nodes were still enlarged.

After the biopsy, I waited eight grueling days for the results. The physician, who was also my colleague and friend, told me the findings indicated that I had a form of B-cell non-Hodgkin lymphoma.

This was not easy to ingest, but I didn’t want to burden her with the heaviness of this diagnosis and began joking around. I was blasé and asked her, “How are we going to fix my leg so that I can run?” refusing to fall apart on the phone.

Then I hung up and broke down in front of my husband. Suddenly, all the plans I had spent my life making seemed irrelevant. Questions revolved in my head. What would happen to my family? My future? My career?

The next step I took was to do what I knew how to do, which was research my diagnosis to find out everything that I could about it. I wanted to know the prognosis, treatment options, statistics, anything to help me wrap my head around this.

What I discovered in this process was much more difficult to accept. There were things that I could not control.

I was driven to face the possibility that I may not live as long as I had expected. I had to accept that I would be living with the uncertainty of my health status and that fighting incurable cancer might become a permanent fixture in my life.

For someone who had devoted decades to building goals and marking off boxes, this was terrifying. There were no checklists for this.

I took time to process what I was facing and came to the recognition that I had a choice – not about living with cancer, but about living my life.

I was not going to allow this diagnosis to define me; I had a strong desire to live my life to the fullest with purpose and direction. I still wanted to work as a nurse practitioner for as long as I could. I still wanted to pursue medical humanitarian work. I still wanted to travel. I still wanted to be active.

In January, I was accepted into a clinical trial for immunotherapy.

Before treatment began, I did something that probably sounded slightly unreasonable to the people around me.

I signed up for Ironman 70.3 Hawaii on June 1, 2024.

I asked my oncologist and the lead investigator of my clinical trial whether I could participate. They told me that I was the first patient they knew of to ask about competing in a half Ironman while undergoing treatment.

They gave me their blessing with one caveat: It would be essential to listen to my body. This was going to be physically taxing on my body due to the cancer already exhausting my body and undergoing treatment in addition to the rigors of training for a 70.3.

I understood the risks. I also understood something else: Training gave me a goal and purpose to keep moving.

Although cancer had taken away my perception of control, it hadn’t taken away my ability to choose a direction.

I arrived in Kona on May 30, 2024. I was nervous and exhausted. I had received treatment the week before and was still recovering from it.

My younger brother, who had agreed to race with me when I asked, and I gathered our swag and attended the informational session. As we were walking out, I saw the iconic walls of all the participants, their bib numbers, and the “why” wall. Seeing my “why” story on the wall solidified my resolve in what I was doing.

The day before the race, I started receiving text messages with their pictures attached, early in the morning, from friends, family, and my whole care team from Fred Hutchinson Cancer Center, wearing a T-shirt that had my cancer ribbon with the words “Fight Cancer Like a Girl” on the front and “Team Rebecca 01” on the back. My amazing husband made nearly 100 shirts and sent them out, asking them to send me a picture and words of encouragement the day before the race. I felt so much love and support from the people in my life.

On June 1, 2024, I stood on the beach waiting for my wave to start at Ironman 70.3 Hawaii. I remember feeling incredibly grateful to be there and have the ability to push my body to the limits.

The race was hard; the winds were cranking so hard that several cyclists were blown into the lava rocks along the highway. It took some serious grit to keep going, but with my husband and kids driving by with my favorite songs blasting on the stereo, cheering me on, I was able to finish the bike.

By the time I started the run, it was midday, and the sun was searing, with temperatures climbing above 90 degrees. My body was fatigued, and every part of me wanted the race to be done. I dug deep. My mantra was “I can do hard things” on repeat. I kept moving.

During those final miles, rolling thoughts in my head surfaced: If I can fight cancer, I can finish this race.

There was an alteration in my thinking and in the purpose of that day; this was no longer about the race but rather an understanding that I was capable of moving forward.

I crossed the finish line with my brother beside me and my family waiting and cheering. I was overwhelmed by my emotions as I crossed the line.

My body was spent; however, there was something strangely beautiful about that exhaustion. I would rather be exhausted from swimming, cycling, and running than exhausted from lying still, consumed by the knowledge that I have incurable cancer.

My training looks different than it used to. Some days are hard, and my body needs downtime.

I’ve learned that listening to my body isn’t a weakness, or even optional at times, but rather a way for me to keep up the fight.

With every step I take, every mile I cycle, every lap that I swim, it is more than just training; it serves as a reminder that my body is still mine and truly a gift to be cherished.

My cancer diagnosis has altered my relationship with time. I functioned at a faster pace and was always thinking ahead about what was next: the next race, the next degree, the next goal, the next item to add to my list.

I am more focused on the mile that I am in right now. I have not stopped making plans; I just hold them differently.

My desires to work, travel, race, and serve others remain, but their priority and intensity are more flexible, as my health and energy can be fluid.

This diagnosis has shifted my perspective – I no longer believe that my worth or value depends on completing every goal I write down.

Some days a win is simply to get out the door. Sometimes it is a slow swim, and the distance may be shorter than planned. Sometimes it is walking instead of running. Sometimes it is resting.

And sometimes, it is standing at a finish line I wasn’t sure I would ever reach.

The trajectory of my life feels unsure and more constrained. However, I know that running, swimming, and cycling are things I can do at the moment, and they make me feel alive.

Honestly, for me, that’s what endurance sport has become. This is not a means of escape from cancer but rather my way of living despite it.

One step, one mile, one breath, I will keep going.

Claude’s new Personal Finance tool aims to help analyze your spending – read-only, of course

📅 2026-11-05  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Anthropic has released what appears to be its answer to ChatGPT Finances.

You can now share your financial information with Claude and ask the chatbot for insights on your spending.

Also: Researchers asked people to use AI chatbots for election info

Personal Finance in Claude, which Anthropic announced Thursday, reviews your connected accounts through read-only access via Plaid. That security measure follows OpenAI’s equivalent feature, ChatGPT Finances, which launched in May. Here’s what to know.

Functionally, this feature appears similar to ChatGPT Finances: It lets you link as many bank and credit card accounts as you’d like to Claude via Plaid with read-only access. You can then ask Claude where you spent your money this month or whether you can afford to take a vacation in six months, for example.

(Disclosure: Ziff Davis, ZDNET’s parent company, filed an April 2025 lawsuit against OpenAI, alleging it infringed Ziff Davis copyrights in training and operating its AI systems.)

Also: Stop sharing your passwords with AI agents

According to Anthropic, the feature is a response to existing use patterns: the company’s April usage report found that financial advice was one of the main topics users ask Claude for help with.

Given the privacy concerns around sharing sensitive data with AI systems, especially agents, Anthropic emphasized which data the new feature will access and how.

“For the accounts you choose to connect (and only those), Claude sees the bank’s name, each account’s name, type, and last four digits, balances and credit limits, and up to two years of transactions. When you chat with Claude in the Personal Finance hub, those conversations aren’t used to train our models.”

That last part is important: Personal Finance in Claude is its own mode with its own protections. If you share or discuss your financial data with Claude outside that “hub,” it’s subject only to your Claude privacy settings.

The idea behind the feature is that you can ask follow-up questions and request more insights about spending habits, trends over time, and future goals based on what Claude finds, similar to any other Claude conversation. The tool will also generate tables and charts to visualize your financial information.

“You can ask for a weekly spending chart, a calendar of what’s due, or a dashboard of the numbers you choose, and Claude builds it as an artifact you can keep adjusting and coming back to,” an Anthropic representative told ZDNET via email.

Also: I connected ChatGPT to my bank, and it’s my go-to finance app now

You can also expect an ad-free experience in the feature.

“Like the rest of Claude, Personal Finance has no ads or sponsored recommendations, and it doesn’t offer credit cards, loans, or savings accounts of its own, so your money questions get a straight answer,” the company said in the announcement.

The company added that because Claude is using actual transaction data and not memory, the feature shouldn’t hallucinate.

“It can list the exact transactions behind any number so you can check its math,” Anthropic said via email. “Like any AI, it can make mistakes, so for anything important, we recommend confirming with your bank.”

Still, the difference — and for many, the appeal — here is the natural language interface. Other finance apps don’t necessarily have an easy, accessible chat function where users can shoot off quick questions like they would to a human financial planner. These assistants also present historical budget analysis in plain English, rather than requiring users to interpret dashboards themselves, which users may see as a perk.

Also: 5 ways to use AI when your budget is tight

On Reddit, many users find ChatGPT Finances to be accurate and reliable. One Reddit user said ChatGPT Finance seemed to have Mint or Monarch potential, but noted it was still “weaker” than those apps in some areas, such as historical syncing, high-quality dashboards, and generating full statement PDFs.

ZDNET editor-in-chief Elyse Betters Picaro was “incredibly impressed” with ChatGPT Finances when she tried it out, and still uses it as one of several financial tools (not a replacement for professional advice). She also has her privacy settings in ChatGPT structured to reduce any security risks.

“ChatGPT has genuinely helped me make better financial decisions,” she wrote.

Anthropic noted that users shouldn’t look to Personal Finance for Claude as a total authority. “Personal Finance is a first step. For life’s bigger money decisions, we think people are best served by Claude working alongside the licensed professionals and regulated firms they trust,” the company said in the announcement.

“We’re exploring how to make that possible in Claude, and we’d like to hear from banks, wealth managers, and financial services companies who want to help shape it” — indicating a more integrated financial planning system within Claude may be on the way.
